Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **William Perkins' Life and Legacy**
	+ Perkins was a professor of biblical and systematic theology at Westminster Theological Seminary.
	+ He was a renowned preacher and theologian who died at 44 in 1602.
	+ He was a key figure in the Puritan movement and a leading theologian of his time.
* **Perkins' Works and Their Rarity**
	+ Perkins' works are rare and have not been widely available for study.
	+ This is despite his significant contributions to Puritan theology and his influence on the English Puritan movement.
	+ His works have been translated into several languages, including Dutch, German, French, and Hungarian.
* **The Publication of Perkins' Complete Works**
	+ A complete edition of Perkins' works is being published over the next decade.
	+ The publication will be done in a way that allows for a sincere attempt to understand and apply the truths in each volume.
	+ The editors are Joel R. Beeke and Derek W. H. Thomas.
* **The Significance of Perkins' Theology**
	+ Perkins was a master preacher and teacher of Reformed, experiential theology.
	+ His writings were influential in shaping the English Puritan movement.
	+ He wrote extensively on predestination, as well as other doctrinal and practical subjects.
* **The History of Perkins' Works**
	+ The 1631 edition of his English Works filled over 2,000 pages in three folio volumes.
	+ Despite their significance, Perkins' full Works have not been in print since the early seventeenth century.
	+ Ian Breward's work helped to promote the study of Perkins, but his works are now rare.
* **The Publication of Volume 3**
	+ Volume 3 of Perkins' Works is a commentary on Hebrews 1-11.
	+ It is edited by Randall J. Pederson and Ryan M. Hurd.
	+ The publication is part of the Reformation Heritage Books series.
* **The Editors and Publishers**
	+ The general editors of the series are Joel R. Beeke and Derek W. H. Thomas.
	+ The publisher is Reformation Heritage Books, based in Grand Rapids, Michigan.
	+ The publisher's address and contact information are provided.

• **Publication of William Perkins' Works**
	+ A single-volume compilation of Perkins' writings was published in 1970, but it only included samplings of his work.
	+ Reformation Heritage Books is now publishing Perkins' Works in a newly typeset format.

• **Modernization of Text**
	+ The text has been modernized to conform to modern American standards.
	+ Spelling and capitalization have been updated, but the original words and word order have been retained.

• **Changes to Quotations and Citations**
	+ Perkins did not use quotation marks to distinguish direct and indirect quotations.
	+ The editors have removed italics and replaced them with quotation marks.

• **Scripture Quotations**
	+ Perkins quoted the Geneva Bible, and the editors have left his quotations in his original words.
	+ Scripture references in the margins have been brought into the text and enclosed in square brackets.

• **Notes and Footnotes**
	+ Parenthetical Scripture references have been abbreviated and punctuated according to modern custom.
	+ Other notes from the margins have been placed in footnotes and labeled "In the margin".

• **Volume Breakdown**
	+ The projected Works of William Perkins will include 10 volumes.
	+ The volumes will cover biblical exposition, doctrinal and polemical treatises, and ethical and practical writings.

• **Theology of William Perkins**
	+ Perkins defined theology as the science of living blessedly forever.
	+ His theology focused on applying theological concepts to daily life.

• **Perkins' Contributions**
	+ Perkins was a Reformed theologian who made significant contributions to dogmatic theology.
	+ His greatest achievement was his ability to apply theological concepts to pastoral issues.

• **Perkins' Style**
	+ Perkins' comments on biblical texts were characterized by experiential warmth and a desire to address pastoral issues.
	+ His examination of Hebrews 11:1-12:1 is a prime example of his skill in handling pastoral issues.

• **Editorial Team**
	+ The editors of the Works of William Perkins include Joel R. Beeke and Derek W. H. Thomas.
	+ They have written an introduction to each volume to orient the reader to its contents.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Origin of the Commentary**
	+ Written for a specific audience (Great St. Andrew's Church)
	+ Preached as sermons, later transcribed and edited
	+ Heavily sermonic undertone and repetitive nature due to its origins

• **Style and Approach**
	+ Proceeds close to the words of the Epistle, but refrains from direct Greek recourse
	+ Focuses on derived doctrine and application rather than textual minutiae
	+ Simple and plain style, rarely delving into textual issues

• **Exegesis and Textual Issues**
	+ Perkins pays attention to exegetical matters, but not extensively
	+ Notes textual issues, such as the difference between "tempted" and "burned"
	+ Tends to go wide rather than deep into the text of Scripture

• **Scriptural Quotations**
	+ Derived from the Tomson revision of the Geneva Bible and the Bishop's Bible
	+ Often paraphrased or accommodated to suit the point at hand
	+ Variances in style and sentence structure suggest some revision

• **Editorial Revision**
	+ Posthumous editorial revision may have introduced variances in main headings
	+ Variances within the text itself likely belong to Perkins
	+ Revision was likely kept to a minimum to preserve the original text

• **Structure and Organization**
	+ Offers a separate chapter or sermon for virtually every verse of the chapter
	+ Uses a recommended pattern for sermons, as noted by Muller

• **Biblical Roots**
	+ Perkins' uses are remarkably and consistently rooted in the text at hand
	+ Despite repetition, the commentary remains biblically grounded

• **Commentary Characteristics**
	+ A textual commentary that proceeds close to the words of the Epistle
	+ Focuses on derived doctrine and application rather than textual minutiae
	+ Simple and plain style, rarely delving into textual issues

• **Perkins' Approach to Hebrews 11**
	+ Examines the lives and backgrounds of the figures in Hebrews 11
	+ Reveals a tendency to go wide rather than deep into the text of Scripture
	+ Uses the material to explore themes and applications

• **Overall Assessment**
	+ Perkins' commentary on Hebrews 11 is a valuable resource for understanding Puritan pietism
	+ Offers a unique perspective on the text, shaped by its sermonic origins
	+ Remains a relevant and insightful commentary on the Epistle to the Hebrews.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **Perkins' Commentary on Hebrews 11**
	+ Breaks up the text thematically, focusing on individual figures
	+ Covers chapters on Abraham and Moses, as well as other verses
* **Perkins' Method of Commentary**
	+ Extracts doctrine from the text and interweaves practical uses
	+ Begins with an exposition of Hebrews 11:1, "Now faith is the ground of things which are hoped for: and the evidence of things not seen"
* **Three Aspects of Faith**
	+ Historical faith: assenting to the truths of the Christian religion
	+ Miraculous faith: being persuaded to be used by God for a great purpose
	+ Saving faith: persuasion of one's salvation in Christ, given by the Holy Spirit
* **Saving Faith**
	+ Concerns persuasion of one's salvation in Christ
	+ Gives assurance of blessings, making them seem already possessed
* **Faith as Ground of Hope**
	+ Hope consists of justification, sanctification, resurrection, glorification, and life everlasting
	+ Faith convinces the mind and understanding of the truth and reliability of God's promises
* **Christ's Presence in the Old Testament**
	+ Believers in the Old Testament were truly partakers of the body and blood of Christ through saving faith
	+ Saving faith makes things that are not in nature to have a being and subsistence
* **The Lord's Supper**
	+ Perkins corrects the Roman Catholic belief that the bread and wine become the actual body and blood of Christ
	+ Interjects a discussion of the Lord's Supper in his exposition of Hebrews 11
* **Faith in Times of Doubt**
	+ Faith can restore a person who has lost a sense of God's favor
	+ Perkins writes that faith can make one's sense of God's favor more comfortable and more earnestly loved
* **True Faith and Assurance**
	+ True faith consists of certainty and assurance
	+ Assurance is of the essence of faith, according to Perkins

	+ The authors express gratitude to those who helped make the publication of the book possible.
	+ They thank the series editors, typists, director of publishing, and typesetters for their contributions.
* **William Perkins' Works**
	+ Perkins' work "A Cloud of Faithful Witnesses" was a popular commentary on the 11th chapter of Hebrews.
	+ It was reprinted multiple times between 1607 and 1631.
	+ The work was a favorite reading of the merchant class in early British North America.
* **Perkins' Influence**
	+ Perkins' doctrine of predestination is discussed in various scholarly works.
	+ His influence on Reformed theology is acknowledged, but caution is advised against overemphasizing his impact.
* **Perkins' Significance**
	+ Charles Hambrick-Stowe considers Perkins the greatest theologian of the Puritan movement.
	+ Perkins and William Ames helped lay the foundation of German Pietism.
* **Perkins' Legacy**
	+ Puritanism at its core was its own form of Pietism, with Perkins as its greatest publicist.
	+ German Pietism diverged from earlier movements, but exhibited no special fullness of type.
* **Perkins' Originality**
	+ Perkins was the progenitor and originator of Puritan Pietism.
	+ His work was influential in shaping the development of Pietism.
* **Perkins' Influence on Casuistry**
	+ Perkins' work on cases of conscience showed no indication of consulting papistical precursors.
	+ Protestant casuists rejected much of Jesuit casuistry, but still drew from it.
* **Perkins' Reception**
	+ Perkins' work was widely read and influential in early British North America.
	+ His work was also influential in Germany, where it was received and translated.
* **Scholarly Works**
	+ Various scholars have written about Perkins' life and work, including Richard A. Muller and Leif Dixon.
	+ Their works provide insight into Perkins' doctrine of predestination and his influence on Reformed theology.
* **Conclusion**
	+ The authors express gratitude to those who helped make the publication of the book possible.
	+ They acknowledge the significance of Perkins' work and its ongoing influence on theology and Pietism.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **William Perkins' Background**
	+ Perkins was an English Puritanist who lived from 1558 to 1602
	+ He was influenced by Roman Catholic casuistry in his practical divinity
* **Perkins' Relationship with Puritanism**
	+ Perkins did not identify as a Puritan, but did not dismiss their efforts for inward purity
	+ He distanced himself from Presbyterians and wanted to continue exercising his ministry in conformity with the established Church
* **Perkins' Role in the Church of England**
	+ Perkins was an apologist for the Church of England, particularly during its emergence from the English Reformation
	+ He never formally criticized the English church or its ministry
* **Perkins' Views on Visual Representation**
	+ He rejected depicting the Godhead within the church service
	+ He allowed biblical representations for civil and domestic use
* **Perkins' Personal Life**
	+ He lived an idle, carefree, and sinful life as a student
	+ He came from an affluent family that could fund his leisurely lifestyle
* **Perkins' Conversion and Preaching**
	+ He converted to a more devout life and consistently preached against idleness and worldly enrichment
	+ He believed that material prosperity was not a sign of God's favor, but rather a means to an end
* **Perkins' Views on Wealth and Poverty**
	+ He believed that one could take and use abundance if it was given without seeking it
	+ He did not hate the poor, but rather saw material prosperity as a sign of God's favor for the individual, not the poor
* **Perkins' Legacy**
	+ He was a prominent figure in the development of Protestant England
	+ His writings and teachings continue to be studied and debated by scholars today

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **William Perkins' views on poverty**
	+ Poverty was seen as an indicator of sin, failure, and damnation
	+ Perkins did not promote a worldly materialism, but believed wealth should be used for the good of others
* **Perkins' views on conversion**
	+ Conversion was a gradual process, not a sudden transformation
	+ It could take a long time to complete, consisting of 10 stages, 4 preparatory and 6 gracious
* **Perkins' casuistry**
	+ He developed a system of casuistry, which was a method of resolving moral dilemmas
	+ His casuistry was influential in the development of Puritan thought
* **Perkins' commentary on Hebrews**
	+ He wrote a commentary on Hebrews 11, which was published in 1609
	+ His commentary was influential in the development of Protestant exegesis
* **Perkins' views on popish doctrine**
	+ He repeatedly reproved popish doctrine in his writings
	+ His views on popish doctrine were influential in English Protestant thought
* **Perkins' views on faith and assurance**
	+ He believed that faith and assurance were essential components of conversion
	+ He wrote extensively on the topic of faith and assurance in his commentary on Hebrews
* **Perkins' views on the role of wealth**
	+ He believed that wealth should be used for the good of others, not for personal enrichment
	+ He saw wealth as a means of serving God and others
* **Perkins' influence on Puritan thought**
	+ He was a key figure in the development of Puritan thought and practice
	+ His writings and ideas influenced many other Puritan theologians and thinkers
* **Perkins' views on the nature of conversion**
	+ He believed that conversion was a gradual process, not a sudden transformation
	+ He saw conversion as a process of growth and development in the Christian life
* **Perkins' legacy**
	+ He was a influential theologian and writer in the English Reformation
	+ His ideas and writings continue to be studied and influential today

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Importance of Following Examples**
	+ The author encourages readers to follow the examples of those who have gone before them in faith
	+ This is based on the idea that seeing the faith of others can be a powerful motivator and comfort

• **The Example of Moses**
	+ Moses' desire to be led by God's presence in the wilderness, even if it meant not entering the promised land
	+ This shows that even the greatest leaders of the past valued God's presence above His promises

• **The Role of the Cloud of Witnesses**
	+ The cloud of witnesses is a group of believers who have gone before us in faith
	+ They serve as a pledge of God's presence and a direction for those who follow in their footsteps

• **The Value of Seeing Faith in Action**
	+ Seeing the faith of others in action can be a powerful way to experience the comfort of God's truth
	+ This is because we are prone to doubt and need to see evidence of God's presence in the lives of others

• **The Need for Faith in Action**
	+ Faith that is not put into practice is not true faith
	+ We must see faith in action in the lives of others in order to be motivated to put our own faith into practice

• **The Importance of Noticing the End of Others' Conversations**
	+ The author encourages readers to consider the end of the conversations of those who have gone before them in faith
	+ This means looking at the outcome of their faith and seeing how it has been rewarded

• **The Role of the Cloud of Witnesses in Our Lives**
	+ The cloud of witnesses serves as a reminder of God's presence and a direction for our own lives
	+ It provides us with a pattern of faith to follow and a motivation to put our own faith into practice

• **The Need for Faith in All Estates of Life**
	+ Faith is necessary in all estates of life, not just in times of prosperity
	+ We must live by faith, not by sight, in order to please God

• **The Danger of Hypocrisy**
	+ Those who begin in the spirit but end in the flesh are hypocrites
	+ Their faith is not genuine and will not support them in times of need

• **The Importance of Putting Faith into Practice**
	+ We must see faith in action in the lives of others in order to be motivated to put our own faith into practice
	+ This means putting our faith into practice in our own lives and living by faith, not by sight.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **The purpose of the chapter**
	+ To persuade the Hebrews to persevere in faith unto the end
	+ To suffer patiently whatever shall befall them in the profession of faith
* **The reason for the exhortation**
	+ The excellency of faith is the main reason for the exhortation
	+ Faith is an excellent gift of God
* **The scope of the chapter**
	+ To urge the Hebrews to persevere and continue in faith
	+ To persuade them of the excellency of faith
* **The structure of the chapter**
	+ Two main parts: a general description of faith and an illustration of faith through examples
	+ The description of faith consists of three actions or effects of faith
* **The three effects of faith**
	+ Faith makes things which are not (but only are hoped for) after a sort to subsist and be present with the believer
	+ Faith makes a believer approved of God
	+ Faith makes a man understand and believe things incredible to sense and reason
* **The meaning of faith**
	+ Faith is the ground of things which are hoped for, and the evidence of things which are not seen
	+ Faith is a special persuasion wrought by the Holy Ghost in the heart of those that are effectually called
* **Types of faith**
	+ Historical faith: a knowledge of the word and an assent of the heart to the truth of it
	+ Miraculous faith: an inward persuasion of the heart wrought by the Holy Ghost
	+ Saving faith: a special persuasion wrought by the Holy Ghost in the heart of those that are effectually called
* **The main type of faith in this chapter**
	+ Saving faith (justifying or saving faith) is the principal type of faith meant in this place
* **The general scope of the chapter**
	+ The chapter is principally about saving faith, which is the faith that saves a man
* **The importance of perseverance in faith**
	+ Perseverance in faith is a matter of great necessity and excellency, and the author of this Epistle uses a large and forcible exhortation to urge it.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Definition of Faith**
	+ Faith is a saving faith that requires careful instruction and understanding.
	+ It is the ground or substance of things hoped for and not seen.

• **Nature of Faith**
	+ Faith gives a being or substance to things that are not yet present.
	+ It gives a present being in the heart of the believer to things that are not yet seen.

• **Things Faith is Ground or Substance For**
	+ Things hoped for and not seen.
	+ These include things that were hoped for by the fathers of the Old Testament and are also hoped for by us.

• **Examples of Things Hoped For**
	+ Incarnation of Christ
	+ Publishing of the gospel to the Jew and the Gentile
	+ Justification (standing in the remission of sins)
	+ Sanctification in this life
	+ Perfection and accomplishment of sanctification after this life
	+ Resurrection of the body and reuniting it with the soul
	+ Glorification of body and soul
	+ Life everlasting and glory with God in heaven

• **Power of Faith**
	+ Faith gives a certain assurance of things hoped for.
	+ It makes these things seem present to the believer, even if they are not yet seen.

• **Evidence of Faith**
	+ Faith convinces the mind, understanding, and judgment to believe the promises of God certainly.
	+ It compels the believer to accept the promises of God as true.

• **Representation of Invisible Things**
	+ Faith takes invisible things and represents them to the heart of the believer.
	+ It makes the believer see and enjoy these invisible things, even if they are not yet present.

• **Conception of Invisible Things**
	+ Faith conceives of invisible things as being really present to the view of the believer.
	+ It allows the believer to rejoice in the sight and enjoyment of these invisible things.

• **Example of Faith**
	+ Faith convinces the believer that life everlasting is certain to come to pass.
	+ It makes the believer see and enjoy life everlasting, even if it is not yet present.

• **Property of Faith**
	+ Faith has the power to take invisible things and represent them to the heart of the believer.
	+ It makes the believer see and enjoy these invisible things, even if they are not yet present.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Faith makes the invisible visible**
	+ Faith represents the joys of eternal life to the soul
	+ The soul enjoys this life everlasting, even in comparison to the present world
	+ Faith makes the present absent and the manifest invisible

• **Faith is a most excellent evidence of things not seen**
	+ Faith gives a being to things that are not yet seen
	+ Faith assures the judgment that these things will come to pass
	+ Faith represents these things to the soul in lively and joyful ways

• **The first effect of faith**
	+ Faith gives a substance and being to things that are not
	+ Faith makes things that are not in nature to have a being and subsistence

• **Faith in the Old Testament**
	+ The fathers in the Old Testament were truly partakers of the body and blood of Christ
	+ This is possible through the power of saving faith, which makes things present to the believer

• **Christ's presence in the Old Testament**
	+ Christ is a lamb slain from the beginning of the world (Rev 13:8)
	+ Christ's death is present to the believer through faith, even before it occurred in time

• **Demonstration of faith in John 8:56**
	+ Abraham saw Christ and rejoiced, even though Christ was not born for many years
	+ Abraham's faith made Christ present to him in a lively and joyful way

• **Faith makes the absent present**
	+ The ancient Israelites ate the same spiritual bread and drank the same spiritual rock (1 Cor 10:3)
	+ They received Christ as lively and effectually through their faith, even before He came

• **Faith gives right and title to Christ**
	+ The fathers in the Old Testament received Christ through faith
	+ Their faith gave them a right and title to Christ, and they felt the efficacy of His death and resurrection

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Visible Church**
	+ The visible church is divided into two sorts: those who lived before the flood and those who lived after the flood.
	+ The text focuses on three faithful men who lived before the flood: Abel, Enoch, and Noah.

• **Abel's Faith**
	+ Abel's faith is commended for three things:
		- He offered a greater sacrifice than Cain.
		- He obtained testimony with God.
		- He is still speaking through his faith, even though he is dead.

• **Abel's Sacrifice**
	+ The ordinary interpretation of Abel's sacrifice is that he offered by faith, while Cain did not.
	+ However, the correct interpretation is that Abel's faith motivated him to offer the best and costliest sacrifice, while Cain offered only what was convenient.

• **The Three Points of Abel's Sacrifice**
	+ First, Cain and Abel both offered sacrifices to God.
	+ Second, they both offered sacrifices, but in different ways.
	+ Third, Abel's sacrifice was better than Cain's.

• **How Cain and Abel Learned to Worship God**
	+ They learned from their parents, Adam and Eve, who received a covenant of grace from God.
	+ Adam and Eve believed the promise and taught their children how to worship God aright.

• **The Importance of Parental Instruction**
	+ Parents should learn from Adam's example and teach their children the ways of God.
	+ This includes instructing them in the proper way to offer sacrifices to God.

• **The Role of Faith in Worship**
	+ Faith is the key to proper worship, as seen in Abel's example.
	+ Without faith, worship is not acceptable to God.

• **The Difference between Abel and Cain**
	+ Abel's faith motivated him to offer a better sacrifice than Cain.
	+ Cain's lack of faith led him to offer a sacrifice that was not pleasing to God.

• **The Importance of Faith in Salvation**
	+ Faith is the means of salvation, as seen in Abel's example.
	+ Without faith, salvation is not possible.

• **The Commendation of Abel's Faith**
	+ Abel's faith is commended as an example of true faith.
	+ His faith is seen as a model for all believers to follow.

• **Abel's Sacrifice**
	+ Abel's sacrifice was commended because it was a better sacrifice than Cain's
	+ Abel brought the best and fattest of his sheep, showing he would have given more if he could

	+ The practice of Abel's sacrifice was later written into law (Ex. 34:19, Lev. 23:10, Deut. 15:21)
	+ The law commanded that only the best and unblemished animals be offered to God

• **The Equity of the Law**
	+ Although the ceremonial law is ended in Christ, its equity and use still apply to us
	+ The law teaches us to give the best we have when offering anything to God

• **Duties Taught by Abel's Sacrifice**
	+ **To the Parent**: Consecrate your best child to the Lord for His service in the ministry
	+ **To the Young Man**: Give your young years to God, not your old age
	+ **To All Christians**: Offer the best you have, not just by halves or for show

	+ God will not accept a rotten sacrifice of old age, but rather give you up to the devil
	+ Remember Abel's sacrifice and offer the best you have

• **The Devil's Policy**
	+ The devil's policy is to dishonor God and cast away souls by giving them the worst years of their life
	+ This is a vile policy that should be rejected

• **Solomon's Counsel**
	+ Remember thy Creator in the days of thy youth (Eccl. 12:1)
	+ This is the counsel of Solomon, who bids us remember God in our youth

• **The Perpetual Honor of Abel**
	+ Abel's sacrifice was honored by God and became a law
	+ His practice is still remembered and taught to this day as a worthy deed

• **The Binding Nature of the Law**
	+ The ceremonial law is ended in Christ, but its equity and use still apply to us
	+ We are still bound by the principle of giving the best we have when offering anything to God

• **The Lesson of Abel's Sacrifice**
	+ Abel's sacrifice teaches us to give the best we have, not just by halves or for show
	+ It teaches us to remember God in our youth and offer the best we have to Him.

* **The Relationship Between Person and Actions**
	+ A person's actions are justified by their person, not the other way around.
	+ This is a fundamental point of our religion.
	+ We must look to Christ's righteousness for justification and sanctification.
* **The Condemnation of Justification by Works**
	+ Justification by works is a vain thing, as we must first justify our person before our works can be just.
	+ This is a sufficient reason against justification by works.
	+ We must look to Christ's righteousness for justification, not our own works.

	+ We must renounce all bypaths and follow Christ's doctrine.

	+ If we walk in this way, it will bring us to heaven.

	+ This is because God looks at the heart, not just the external actions.

	+ God's testimony is a sign of His acceptance of a person or their work.
	+ In the Old Testament, this often took the form of fire from heaven.

• **The Greater Testimony of the New Testament**
	+ In the New Testament, God's testimony is not limited to visible signs.
	+ Instead, it is the presence of the Holy Spirit in the heart of a believer that is the greatest testimony of God's acceptance.

• **The Fire of God's Spirit**
	+ The Holy Spirit comes down into the hearts of believers, burning up sin and corruption.

• **The Use of God's Testimony**
	+ The testimony of God's acceptance is a reminder to focus on the heart's condition rather than outward actions.

• **The Importance of Faith in Action**
	+ Faith is not just a theoretical concept, but a living reality that affects every action.
	+ When faith is present, even the most mundane actions can be considered acceptable to God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Example of Abel**
	+ Abel's faith is an example of speaking to God with confidence
	+ His voice continues to speak until the end of the world

• **The Example of Enoch**
	+ Enoch's faith is an example of being translated to heaven without seeing death
	+ He was taken away by God miraculously, not by an ordinary work
	+ His translation was a reward for his faith

• **The Meaning of Enoch's Translation**
	+ Enoch was taken away from earth to heaven, not by death, but miraculously
	+ He was not to see death, meaning he was not to experience dissolution of soul and body
	+ His translation was a high privilege granted to him by God

• **Two Opinions on Enoch's Translation**
	+ Some think Enoch was translated in soul only, not in body
	+ They argue that no unglorified mortal body can enter heaven

• **Response to the First Reason**
	+ The glorification of Enoch's body is implied in the text
	+ If his body did not see death, it was made immortal, which is a part of glorification

• **Response to the Second Reason**
	+ Christ was the first to enter heaven in body and soul, but Enoch never died
	+ Therefore, Enoch could be in heaven in his body before Christ's human flesh ascended

• **Response to the Third Reason**
	+ The passage in John is not meant to be taken literally, but rather as a statement about Christ's unique understanding of heavenly mysteries
	+ Enoch's translation is not a physical ascension, but a miraculous translation to heaven

• **Enoch's Faith**
	+ Enoch's faith was in the Messiah to come
	+ He believed steadfastly in the Messiah, which was a reward for his faith

• **The Importance of Faith**
	+ Faith is necessary to please God
	+ Without faith, it is impossible to please God

• **The Example of Enoch's Faith**
	+ Enoch's faith is an example of how to live a life of faith
	+ His faith was rewarded with a miraculous translation to heaven

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **Two Opinions about Enoch's Translation**
	+ Opinion 1: Enoch was translated both in body and soul
	+ Opinion 2: Enoch was taken up in soul and body into paradise, where he lives in a mortal body and will return to fight against Antichrist
* **Refutation of Opinion 2**
	+ The earthly paradise was defaced by the flood and there is no record of anyone entering it after Adam
	+ A mortal body is unclean and cannot enter the heavenly paradise
	+ The text of Ecclesiasticus 44:16 is corrupted and does not support the idea of Enoch being translated into paradise
* **The True and Orthodox Judgment of the Church**
	+ Enoch was assumed into heaven both body and soul, with his soul perfectly sanctified and his body glorified
	+ He remains in glory, expecting the general resurrection and the full glorification of all God's elect
* **Lessons from Enoch's Translation**
	+ There is a life everlasting prepared by God for His children, where they will live forever in soul and body
	+ God is not tied to the order of nature, as seen in Enoch's translation and Elijah's assumption into heaven
* **Comparison between Enoch and Elijah**
	+ Both were assumed into heaven in soul and body and remain there to this day
	+ Both were examples of God's power and provision for His children
* **Implications of Enoch's Translation**
	+ It provides a notable ground for the article of faith in life everlasting
	+ It shows that God is not limited by the natural order and can do extraordinary things
* **The Order of Nature**
	+ God established the order that dust thou art, and to dust shalt thou return after Adam's fall
	+ Enoch's translation shows that God is not bound by this order
* **The Significance of Enoch's Translation**
	+ It demonstrates God's power and provision for His children
	+ It provides a hope for eternal life and the resurrection of the body
* **The Example of Enoch**
	+ He was a holy man who pleased God and was translated into heaven
	+ His example shows that God can do extraordinary things and provide for His children in unexpected ways
* **The Importance of Understanding Enoch's Translation**
	+ It is essential for understanding the nature of God and His relationship with His children
	+ It provides a foundation for the article of faith in life everlasting and the resurrection of the body

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Decree of Death**
	+ All men are subject to death, as decreed by God
	+ No one can escape death, not even with the help of the entire world
	+ However, God is not bound by this decree, as seen in the cases of Enoch and Elijah

• **Enoch and Elijah's Exception**
	+ Enoch and Elijah were taken up by God without experiencing death
	+ Their bodies did not decay, demonstrating God's power over nature
	+ This exception highlights God's sovereignty and authority

• **The Papist Error**
	+ The Catholic Church's doctrine of limbus patrum (a place outside heaven) is false
	+ Enoch and Elijah were in heaven, both in body and soul, before Christ's incarnation
	+ This disproves the Catholic Church's teaching on the subject

• **The Holy Ghost's Proof**
	+ The Holy Ghost proves that Enoch was taken away by God
	+ The proof is based on the testimony of the Old Testament in Genesis 5:14
	+ The Holy Ghost uses this testimony to demonstrate the authority of God's Word

• **The Authority of Scripture**
	+ The Holy Ghost Himself vouches for the authority of Scripture
	+ Teachers should allege Scripture, and hearers should receive it above all human testimonies
	+ This demonstrates the high reverence due to the Holy Scriptures

• **Enoch's Faith**
	+ Enoch pleased God before he was taken away
	+ Without faith, it is impossible to please God (Hebrews 11:6)
	+ Therefore, Enoch was taken away by faith

• **The Degrees of Evidence**
	+ The Holy Ghost frames an argument to prove that Enoch was taken away by faith
	+ The argument consists of three degrees of evidence:
		- God took Enoch away
		- Enoch pleased God before he was taken away
		- Without faith, no one can please God

• **Enoch's Pleasing God**
	+ Enoch pleased God, as evidenced by his walking with God
	+ Walking with God is an assured testimony that he pleased God
	+ This is demonstrated by the prophet Amos's statement that two cannot walk together unless they are agreed

• **The Report of Enoch's Pleasing God**
	+ There is a report or testimony given of Enoch that he pleased God
	+ This report is based on the story of Genesis, where it is said that Enoch walked with God
	+ This testimony is sufficient proof that Enoch pleased God

• **The Conclusion**
	+ Enoch was taken away by faith, as demonstrated by the Holy Ghost's argument
	+ This teaches us that we must seek to please God before we can expect to be translated into heaven
	+ We must learn to please God in this life, not just when the time of translation comes.

* **God's Presence and Judgment**

	+ God will judge us for our actions
* **Living a Godly Life**
	+ When we feel God's presence, we are more likely to live a godly life
	+ We will be more obedient and dutiful
* **Consequences of Not Feeling God's Presence**
	+ We may become careless in our actions and dealings
	+ We may not take heed of our words and actions
* **God's Commandment**
	+ God commands us to "walk before me and be upright" (Genesis 17:1)
	+ This commandment is for all God's children
* **Examples of God's Children**
	+ Enoch, Abraham, and David are examples of those who have walked with God
	+ They have prospered and been blessed as a result
* **Prosperity and Success**
	+ Walking with God will bring us prosperity and success
	+ We will be in God's favor and will succeed in all our endeavors
* **Patience in Affliction**
	+ When we walk with God, we will be more patient in affliction
	+ We will see that God's providence is always present and that He disposes of all things for our good
* **Avoiding Sin**
	+ Walking with God will help us avoid sin
	+ We will be more mindful of our thoughts, words, and actions
* **Joseph's Example**
	+ Joseph's experience shows that walking with God can turn affliction to good
	+ He was able to endure persecution and hardship because he knew he was under God's power
* **Making Conscience of Sin**
	+ Walking with God will help us make conscience of all sin
	+ We will be more aware of our thoughts, words, and actions and will strive to live a holy life

• **The Importance of Walking with God**

	+ Therefore, without faith, no man can be saved or come to heaven

	+ They must lay off ignorance and presumption and labor for a sound and saving faith

• **The Importance of Faith**

	+ Without faith, one cannot please God, no matter how good their intentions may be

• **Scriptures provide sufficient direction for all actions**

	+ Examples of God's ordinary works in His creatures provide excellent rules of direction

• **Scripture provides a comprehensive guide for Christian living**
	+ The Scriptures cover various callings and situations
	+ Rules and examples provide a comprehensive guide for living a Christian life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **Believing in God**
	+ To have fellowship with God in Christ, one must believe in God
	+ This belief is not just that God exists, but that God is the true and only God
* **The Difficulty of Believing in God**
	+ By nature, people do not think the true God is God
	+ People are atheists in their hearts, denying God's power, presence, and justice
* **Denying God's Presence**
	+ People commit sinful actions without shame or fear, implying they do not believe God is present
	+ If they believed God was present, they would be ashamed to commit such actions
* **Denying God's Power**
	+ People do not fear punishment from God, even when they break His laws
	+ They do not believe God has the power to revenge the contempt of His laws
* **Denying God's Justice**
	+ People do not believe God will condemn and punish sin
	+ They persuade themselves they will escape punishment, even when they see others punished for the same sin
* **The Corruption of Human Nature**
	+ People frame God in their own image, making Him a god of their own liking
	+ They deny God's power, presence, and justice, making Him a mere idol
* **The Consequences of Denying God's Justice**
	+ People think they can sin without consequence, even when they see others punished
	+ This leads to a false sense of security and a lack of fear of God's punishment
* **The Importance of Believing in God's Justice**
	+ Believing in God's justice is essential to having a true understanding of God
	+ It requires acknowledging God's power to punish sin and His justice in condemning it
* **The Scope of the First Commandment**
	+ The first commandment is to believe in the true and only God
	+ This commandment is central to Christian religion and requires a deep understanding of God's nature
* **The Challenge of Believing in God**
	+ Believing in God is a matter of great difficulty, even for those who acknowledge His existence
	+ It requires a willingness to acknowledge God's power, presence, and justice, and to submit to His laws.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Men's natural imagination of God**
	+ Men think God is merciful, but not just
	+ They attribute their sins to Christ and God's mercy
	+ They live ignorantly and profanely, without reverence for God's justice

	+ Psalm 10:11-13 describes the wicked saying God has forgotten them
	+ Isaiah 36:18-19 quotes Rabshakeh's blasphemy that God cannot deliver them
	+ Isaiah 28:15 describes the wicked saying they have made a covenant with death

• **Every man is a natural atheist**
	+ By nature, men deny God's presence, power, and justice
	+ They do not believe God is God indeed

• **Four sorts of people who practice atheism**
	+ **First sort**: Those who openly deny God's existence
		- They dispute the question and claim there is no God
		- They are fools and devils incarnate, deserving of hatred and death

• **Second sort: Those who worship a false god**
	+ They acknowledge and worship a god, but a false one
	+ Examples include sun worship, idol worship, and covetousness

• **Third sort: Those who worship the true God in a false manner**
	+ **First subgroup: Turks**
		- They acknowledge the true God, but deny the Trinity and deity of Christ
	+ **Second subgroup: Jews**
		- They acknowledge the true God, but deny the Trinity and deity of Christ
	+ **Third subgroup: True papists**
		- They acknowledge the true God, but deny the Trinity and deity of Christ through their doctrine

• **Papists' doctrine denies Christ's offices**
	+ They deny Christ's true nature as King, Priest, and Prophet
	+ Their doctrine is contrary to the truth and has been published to the world

• **Consequences of atheism**
	+ Those who deny God's existence are traitors to God, mankind, and nature
	+ They deserve death and are to be hated worse than toads and adders

• **Idolatry and covetousness**
	+ Covetousness is idolatry, as the heart is set on riches
	+ Pleasure and the belly can also be idols, as the heart is set on them

• **Conclusion**
	+ Men's natural imagination of God is one of mercy, not justice
	+ Atheism is a common practice, and those who deny God's existence are to be hated and punished.

	+ Those who seek God are rewarded with mercies and favors above their desert.

	+ The promise of God as a rewarder is based on Deuteronomy 32:40-42.
	+ The same phrase is used in both Deuteronomy and the New Testament.
* **Objection and response**
	+ Some may object that the same reward belongs to both seeking and hating God.
	+ The response is that a different reward belongs to each, based on their actions.
* **Worldly men's excuse**
	+ Worldly men may say they hate not God, but are rather good subjects.
	+ The response is that they will still face God's judgment and punishment.
* **Conclusion**
	+ God is a rewarder of those who seek Him and a revenger of those who hate Him.
	+ Those who seek God will be rewarded richly, while those who hate God will face punishment.

* **The Devil's Deception**

	+ Noah's faith and obedience will be highlighted as an example of pleasing God.

	+ We should seek God's service and faith, just as Noah and Enoch did.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's prerogative to reveal His secrets**
	+ God reveals His counsels to those who fear Him.
	+ This is a special privilege given to holy and righteous men.

• **Examples of God's revelation**
	+ To Abraham: God revealed the destruction of Sodom and her sisters.
	+ To prophets: God reveals His secrets to His servants the prophets (Amos 3:7).

• **The secrets of the Lord are among those who fear Him**
	+ Not just prophets, but all true believers who fear God are His friends and part of His council.
	+ God communicates His secrets to them as one friend to another.

• **The prerogative of God's children**
	+ To discern into the deep councils of God (1 Cor. 2:15).
	+ This privilege is not limited to prophets, but is available to all true believers.

• **Cautions regarding this prerogative**
	+ This privilege is more proper to prophets and holy ministers of God than to ordinary Christians.
	+ It was more ordinary in the Old Testament than in the days of the gospel.

• **The state of the church before and after Christ**
	+ The state of the church before Christ was not better than it is now under Christ.
	+ We have the perfect and complete Scriptures, which they did not have.

• **The nature of God's revelation in the Old Testament**
	+ They had more ordinary revelation of matters personal and private.
	+ However, we in the New Testament have more evident demonstration and full revelation of general and salvific matters.

• **The nature of God's revelation in the New Testament**
	+ Revelations of God's will are ordinarily nothing else but the true meaning of Scripture.
	+ This includes discerning true Scripture from forged, true sacraments from supposed, and true doctrine from false.

• **The expectation of God's revelation under the gospel**
	+ True and faithful believers are sure to have revealed unto them from God.
	+ This includes the true meaning of Scripture and discerning true from false.

• **The limitations of God's revelation**
	+ God's revelation is not limited to personal and particular matters.
	+ It is generally limited to what is necessary for salvation.

• **God's Providence**
	+ God's providence is special and extraordinary over His children in great distresses and extremities.
	+ His providence watches over His children like a master watches over his family and cattle.

• **God's Care**
	+ God's care is night and day for His children.
	+ He overlooks all His children always, but especially shows His providence when they are in danger or need.

• **Examples of God's Providence**
	+ Daniel was cast into the lions' den, but God was with him and shut the lions' mouths (Dan. 6:22).
	+ The three children were cast into the fiery furnace, but God was with them and took away the natural force from the fire (3:27).
	+ The Israelites were to pass through the sea, but God was with them and made the sea give place to His children (Ex. 14:22).

	+ Noah's case was pitiful, as he couldn't shut the door of the ark himself.
	+ Others wouldn't help him, but God Himself shut the door for him.

	+ God's eye of love and hand of power were over Noah, and the ark saved him and his household.
	+ God's providence is over His children when they are in the deepest distresses and most destitute of all worldly comforts.

• **Comfort for God's Children**
	+ God's children can be assured of a special care of God over them, even in their greatest extremities.
	+ God will never forget nor forsake them in any of their troubles.

• **Elisha's Experience**
	+ Elisha had an army sent against him, but he was not afraid, knowing that there were more angels for him than against him (2 Kings 6:15-16).

• **Angels and God's Providence**
	+ Angels were sent from God to help Noah, just as there were more angels for Elisha than men in the army against him.
	+ God Himself supplied all the needs of Noah, even when others wouldn't help him.

• **God's Care Today**
	+ God's care is not limited to the past, but is still present over His church and children today.
	+ Though He may not work visible miracles, He is still mighty and wonderful in preserving His children.

• **Trust in God's Providence**
	+ God's children can trust in His providence and care, even in the face of danger and uncertainty.
	+ They can be assured that God will defend them, provide for them, and comfort them in their troubles.

	+ The ark was not a viable means of salvation, as it was designed to withstand the floodwaters but not to provide a safe haven for people.

	+ The text quotes Saint Peter as saying that the spirits of the disobedient are now in prison.
	+ The author suggests that all people were condemned to eternal destruction, but with charity, we can consider the possibility of some repenting.

	+ The author suggests that some people may have repented when they saw the flood coming.
	+ This is based on the idea that some of the descendants of Enoch, Methuselah, and Lamech may have been ashamed of their former unbelief and turned to God.

• **Why Were They Not Saved?**
	+ The author argues that those who repented too late were not saved because they did not repent in time, when they were called by Noah's preaching.
	+ Repentance is never too late to save the soul from hell, but it may be too late to save the body from a temporal judgment.

	+ The author suggests that it is unlikely that none of the descendants of holy patriarchs repented when they saw the flood coming.
	+ This is because they would have heard their fathers preach and may have been influenced by their preaching when the judgment came.

• **Why God Did Not Record Repentance**
	+ The author suggests that God did not record the repentance of some people because He wanted to teach all men to fear disobeying His commandment.
	+ This is to motivate people to repent before it is too late.

	+ The text argues that the story of the flood provides two strong motives for repentance.
	+ If we do not repent in time, our state is fearful and doubtful, and there is great question of our salvation.

	+ The author emphasizes the importance of repenting early, before it is too late.
	+ This is because repentance is never too late to save the soul from hell, but it may be too late to save the body from a temporal judgment.

• **Conclusion**
	+ The text concludes by emphasizing the importance of repentance and the consequences of delaying it.
	+ The story of the flood serves as a warning to all men to fear disobeying God's commandment and to repent before it is too late.

	+ The Church of Rome argues that sanctification is perfect in spirit, soul, and body (1 Thess. 5:23).
	+ However, this is not the case, as sanctification is perfect in parts but not in measure or degree.

• **The Need for Perfect Righteousness**
	+ A man cannot be righteous by the perfect righteousness of the law in himself.
	+ Therefore, we must look to another source of righteousness to be justified.

• **The Source of Righteousness**
	+ The source of righteousness is not our own efforts or imperfect sanctification.
	+ Rather, it is a gift from God, which we receive through faith in Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Problem with Human Righteousness**

	+ Christ's obedience was active (perfect fulfillment of God's will)
	+ Christ's obedience was passive (suffering of God's justice for sin)

• **The Significance of Christ's Righteousness**
	+ Christ's righteousness is the only way to be made righteous before God
	+ It is not based on human works, but on Christ's perfect obedience and purity of nature.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Nature of Righteousness**
	+ Righteousness is perfect when a person's nature is pure and their obedience is perfect.
	+ This is the righteousness that Jesus Christ has, and it is the righteousness that makes a sinner righteous before God.

• **Jesus Christ as the Mediator**
	+ Jesus Christ is the Mediator who has this perfect righteousness.
	+ This righteousness is resident in the holy person of Jesus Christ.

• **The Doctrine of Imputed Righteousness**
	+ The doctrine that one can be justified by another's righteousness is above and beyond human reason.
	+ This doctrine is grounded in God's word and is opposed by the Church of Rome.

• **Proof of Imputed Righteousness from Scripture**
	+ 1 Corinthians 5:21 says that Jesus Christ, who knew no sin, was made sin for us, so that we might be made the righteousness of God in Him.
	+ This passage shows that Christ's righteousness is imputed to us, making us righteous.

• **The Nature of the Righteousness that Saves**
	+ The righteousness that saves us must be the righteousness of both man and God.
	+ No man's own righteousness can make him the righteousness of God, and God's righteousness cannot be the righteousness of man.

• **Christ as the Fulfiller of the Law**
	+ Christ is the end of the law to all who believe (Romans 10:3).
	+ Christ fulfilled the law, not for Himself, but for all who believe.

• **The Relationship between Christ and Believers**
	+ Believers are spiritually and truly conjoined to Christ.
	+ This mystical union between Christ and believers means that the blessings of salvation in Christ are diffused into them.

• **Objection from the Church of Rome**
	+ The Church of Rome objects that a person cannot be righteous by another person's righteousness, just as they cannot be wise or rich by another person's wisdom or riches.

• **Response to the Objection**
	+ The comparison between wisdom, riches, and righteousness is not alike, because we have a right and propriety in Christ's righteousness.
	+ The mystical union between Christ and believers means that Christ's righteousness can be truly ours.

• **Conclusion**
	+ The doctrine of imputed righteousness is grounded in Scripture and is the only way that a sinner can be made righteous before God.
	+ This doctrine is opposed by the Church of Rome, but it is the only way that we can fulfill the law and be made righteous.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Objection 1: God justifies wicked men**
	+ The objection is that if God justifies people through Christ's righteousness, then He is justifying wicked men.
	+ The response is that God does not justify wicked men in their wickedness, but rather in their faith and repentance.

• **Ground of justification**
	+ The ground of justification is Christ's righteousness.
	+ The collection of justification is not the wicked person themselves, but rather the person who believes in Christ and repents of their sins.

• **Justification and sanctification**
	+ Justification is the act of making someone righteous through Christ's righteousness.
	+ Sanctification is the process of making someone holy through the work of God's grace.

• **The believing sinner**
	+ The believing sinner has more righteousness than just Christ's righteousness.
	+ The sinner is justified by Christ's righteousness and sanctified by the work of God's grace.

• **Faith and sanctification**
	+ Faith purifies the heart and leads to sanctification.
	+ A person cannot be justified without being sanctified in their heart and life.

• **Justification and sanctification are distinct**
	+ Justification is perfect because it is based on Christ's inherent righteousness.
	+ Sanctification is imperfect because it is based on the sinner's own efforts.

• **God justifies the ungodly**
	+ God justifies the ungodly by working in them faith and repentance.
	+ This does not mean that the person remains ungodly, but rather that they are made righteous through faith.

• **Christ's righteousness and sin**
	+ Christ's righteousness does not make Him a sinner.
	+ Christ's righteousness is imputed to believers, making them righteous.

• **Christ's role as surety**
	+ Christ is our surety and undertakes for us in our place.
	+ As our surety, Christ is responsible for our sins, but He is not a sinner Himself.

• **The nature of justification**
	+ Justification is not based on the sinner's own righteousness, but rather on Christ's righteousness.
	+ Justification is a gift of God's grace, received through faith in Christ.

	+ Christ is more righteous than all men and angels
	+ His righteousness is imputed to us, making us righteous in God's sight
	+ This righteousness is the only one that can make us righteous in God's sight

• **The Importance of Christ's Humanity**
	+ Christ had to become human to apply His righteousness to us
	+ He had to live, die, and rise again for us to make His righteousness effective

• **The Greatness of God's Goodness**
	+ God put perfect righteousness in Adam's heart in creation
	+ However, Adam lost it, and God gave us another and better righteousness through Christ

• **The Safety of Our Righteousness**
	+ Our righteousness is not in our own keeping, but in Christ's safe hand
	+ We cannot lose our righteousness, even if we sin, because it is in Christ

• **The Comfort of Our Salvation**
	+ Our salvation is not in our own control, but in Christ's safe keeping
	+ We can trust that our righteousness will be preserved and revealed at the last day

• **The Value of Christ's Righteousness**
	+ Christ's righteousness is the only one that can hide our sins from God's justice
	+ It is a glorious mercy seat that covers our filthiness

• **The Importance of Esteeming Ourselves**
	+ We should learn to esteem ourselves and our own natures correctly
	+ We are not righteous in ourselves, but in Christ

• **The Greatness of God's Mercy**
	+ God's mercy is shown in giving us a better righteousness through Christ
	+ He trusts Christ with our salvation, not us

• **The Call to Patience and Comfort**
	+ We should learn patience and be comforted by the knowledge of Christ's righteousness
	+ We can trust that Christ will keep our salvation safe and secure.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Nature of Righteousness**
	+ True righteousness is that which justifies a sinner and makes one righteous
	+ This righteousness is not earned through human effort, but is a gift from God

• **The Source of Righteousness**
	+ This righteousness comes from the person of Christ
	+ It is not something that can be achieved through human means, but is a result of faith in Christ

• **The Instrument of Righteousness**
	+ Faith is the proper instrument created in the soul of man by the Holy Ghost to apprehend this righteousness
	+ Faith is the means by which we receive and apply Christ's righteousness to our souls

• **The Role of Faith**
	+ Faith properly apprehends and applies Christ's righteousness to our souls
	+ Faith is the means by which we receive the promise of salvation and the righteousness of Christ

• **The Manner of Faith's Work**
	+ Faith works in a spiritual and invisible way, but is still a real and effective means of receiving Christ's righteousness
	+ Faith applies Christ's righteousness to our souls in a way that is similar to how a hand takes hold of a garment and applies it to the body

• **How to Know if Faith has Worked**
	+ We know if faith has apprehended and applied Christ's righteousness when we believe that Christ's righteousness is our own and has reconciled us to God
	+ Faith is the means by which we receive justification and reconciliation with God

• **The Uniqueness of Faith**
	+ Faith is the only means by which we can apprehend and apply Christ's righteousness
	+ No other virtue or spiritual power in man's soul can do this

• **Comparison with Other Virtues**
	+ Hope and love are both important virtues, but they do not have the same ability to apprehend and apply Christ's righteousness as faith does
	+ Faith is unique in its ability to receive and apply Christ's righteousness

• **The Importance of Faith**
	+ Faith is essential for receiving and applying Christ's righteousness
	+ Without faith, we cannot receive the promise of salvation and the righteousness of Christ

• **The Exclusive Role of Faith**
	+ Faith alone is able to apprehend and apply Christ's righteousness
	+ No other means or virtue can replace faith in this role.

• **Noah's Faith**

	+ Abraham's obedience is a result of God's calling
	+ He obeyed God's command to leave his native country and go to a new land
	+ He did not know where he was going, but he trusted in God's promise

• **The Cause of Abraham's Faith**
	+ The cause of Abraham's faith is God's calling
	+ God's calling is an action of God that appoints a man to a certain condition or state

• **The Effect of Abraham's Faith**
	+ The effect of Abraham's faith is obedience to God's command
	+ He hearkened and obeyed God's call to leave his native country

• **The Matter of Abraham's Obedience**
	+ The matter of Abraham's obedience was to leave his native country
	+ He went out of his country, not knowing where he was going

• **The End of Abraham's Obedience**
	+ The end of Abraham's obedience was to take possession of a new country
	+ He would not enjoy this country for a long time, but he trusted in God's promise

• **The Manner of Abraham's Obedience**
	+ The manner of Abraham's obedience was to go out, not knowing where he was going
	+ He trusted in God's promise and obeyed His command without knowing the outcome

• **The Significance of Abraham's Faith**
	+ Abraham's faith is an example of the kind of faith that is pleasing to God
	+ His faith is a model for all believers, and his obedience is a result of God's calling

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's Calling of Men**
	+ God calls men to various states of life, including this life and the afterlife
	+ God is compared to a general who assigns every soldier their standing and duty

• **Means of God's Calling**
	+ God calls men two ways: immediately or by means
	+ Immediate calling: God calls directly, as with prophets and apostles
	+ Mediate calling: God calls through men, as with ordinary prophets and priests

• **States of Life**
	+ God's general calling: calls all men to repentance and eternal life
	+ God's particular calling: assigns men to specific duties in family, church, or commonwealth
	+ God's personal calling: assigns men to private and personal duties

• **Abraham's Calling**
	+ Abraham's calling was a private and personal calling to leave his country and family
	+ This calling was not for everyone, but only for Abraham alone

• **Why God Calls Some and Not Others**
	+ God's judgments are wonderful and cannot be understood by human reason
	+ God is not bound by human notions of justice or partiality
	+ God's actions are just and fair, even if they seem unjust or partial to us

• **Circumstances of Abraham's Calling**
	+ Who was called: Abraham, the son of Terah, but not his father or brother
	+ When was Abraham called: the text does not specify the exact time
	+ How was Abraham called: the text does not specify the exact means of the calling

• **God's Mercy and Justice**
	+ God has mercy on whom He will and withholds it from whom He will
	+ God's actions are just and fair, even if they seem unjust or partial to us

• **Human Limitations**
	+ Human reason is shallow and cannot understand God's actions
	+ It is folly and presumption to weigh God's actions in the balance of human reason

• **God's Sovereignty**
	+ God is sovereign and can do what He wills
	+ God's actions are not bound by human notions of justice or fairness

• **Abraham's Unique Calling**
	+ Abraham's calling was a unique and extraordinary calling
	+ This calling was not for everyone, but only for Abraham alone.

• **The Blessings of Obedience**
	+ Obedience to God's commandments can bring blessings and benefits, even in difficult circumstances

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Reasons for Traveling**
	+ To attend a general council or serve as an ambassador

	+ Moses fled to Midian to escape Pharaoh's wrath (Ex. 2:14-15)

	+ The queen of Sheba traveled to Jerusalem to learn from Solomon (2 Kings 10)

	+ Merchants can travel to other nations for trade, as long as they don't compromise their souls

	+ Must not be contrary to God's word
	+ Must not be for frivolous or malicious reasons

• **God's Protection**

	+ Those who travel without God's warrant are outside His protection

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Nature of Suffering**
	+ Suffering is a common experience for God's children across all ages
	+ Christ pronounces blessings on those who suffer persecution for righteousness' sake (Matt. 5:10)

• **The Commandment to Abraham**
	+ The commandment to Abraham to leave his country and family was personally directed to him
	+ However, it has a force and use for Christians today, who must do what is proportionable to it

• **The Soul's Obligation**
	+ The soul of every Christian is the spouse of Christ, the true Solomon
	+ The soul must forget its own kindred and father's house (i.e., its own nature and natural affections) to obey God and serve Christ

• **The End of Obedience**
	+ The end of obedience is to receive an inheritance from God
	+ Abraham's obedience was to leave his country to inherit the land of Canaan, the land of promise

• **The Inheritance of Abraham**
	+ Although Abraham did not personally inherit the land of Canaan, he may be said to have inherited it in two ways:
		- Sacramentally or mystically, as a type of the heavenly Canaan
		- In his posterity, as his descendants inherited the land

• **God's Performance of Promises**
	+ God often gives something equivalent or better than what is promised
	+ In the case of Abraham, God promised the land of Canaan, but gave him the true Canaan, the kingdom of heaven

• **Wisdom in Discerning God's Promises**
	+ We must learn to discern God's merciful performance of His promises
	+ God performs promises not always in the way they are promised, but in a way that is proportionable or better

• **The True Discerning of God's Promises**
	+ God sometimes gives the particular thing promised, as in the case of the children of Israel's deliverance from Egypt
	+ However, He often gives something better, as in the case of Abraham's inheritance of the kingdom of heaven

• **The Reward of Obedience**
	+ Obedience to God's commandments is rewarded with an inheritance from Him
	+ The reward is not always what is promised, but something better or equivalent

• **The Example of Abraham**
	+ Abraham's obedience to God's commandment to leave his country is an example for Christians today
	+ We must do what is proportionable to this commandment, and trust in God's merciful performance of His promises.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's Promises are Not Always Immediate**
	+ God may not always deliver what He promises immediately
	+ Instead, He may give patience and a sense of His favor
	+ This can be more comforting than the actual promise

• **Abraham Inherited Canaan Through His Posterity**
	+ God promised Canaan to Abraham and his seed (Gen. 12:4, 15:7)
	+ However, neither Abraham nor his immediate seed enjoyed it
	+ Instead, his posterity, the Israelites, inherited it 430 years later

• **God's Promises are Not Always Performed to the Same Parties**
	+ God may not always perform His promises to the original recipient
	+ Instead, He may perform them to their descendants or others
	+ This is seen in the case of Abraham and his posterity

• **We Must Wait Patiently for God's Promises**
	+ If God's promises are not fulfilled in our lifetime, we must wait patiently
	+ The prophet Habakkuk says that the vision will come to pass, even if it tarry (Hab. 2:3)
	+ David says that light and joy are sown for the righteous, but not always in fruition (Ps. 97:11)

• **God's Promises are Sown for Future Generations**
	+ Just as a farmer casts seed into the earth, expecting a harvest
	+ God sows promises to His people, expecting them to be fulfilled in future generations
	+ This is seen in the case of Abraham and his posterity

• **The Father May Not Enjoy the Harvest, but the Son Will**
	+ Just as a farmer may die before reaping the harvest
	+ God's promises may not be fulfilled in the lifetime of the original recipient
	+ But their descendants may enjoy the harvest of God's promises

• **Abraham's Faith in God's Promise**
	+ Despite the obstacles, Abraham believed God's promise and obeyed
	+ He knew that God was King of kings and could bring to pass what He had promised
	+ His faith was rewarded, and his posterity inherited the land of Canaan

• **God's Power and Sovereignty**
	+ Abraham knew that God was all-powerful and could overcome any obstacle
	+ He trusted in God's sovereignty and power to bring to pass His promise
	+ This faith was rewarded, and his posterity entered the land of Canaan as conquerors

• **The Conquest of Canaan**
	+ The Israelites, led by Joshua, conquered the land of Canaan
	+ They defeated the mighty kings and their people, and took possession of the land
	+ This was a fulfillment of God's promise to Abraham and his posterity

• **The Importance of Faith and Patience**
	+ Abraham's faith and patience were rewarded, and his posterity inherited the land of Canaan
	+ We must also have faith and patience, trusting in God's sovereignty and power to bring to pass His promises

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's sovereignty over kingdoms**
	+ God can change the state of kingdoms as He pleases.
	+ The welfare and prosperity of a kingdom depend on God's favor, not human policy or might.

• **Prayer for the good estate of the kingdom**
	+ We should pray earnestly for the good estate of our kingdom and its rulers.
	+ The prayers of godly ministers are like the horses and chariots of Israel.

• **The ruin of kingdoms**
	+ Sin and ungodliness are the causes of the downfall of kingdoms.
	+ God does not overthrow a state without cause, but rather exercises His justice.

• **The example of the Canaanites**
	+ God took the land from the Canaanites because of their abominable sins.
	+ The wickedness of the Amorites was not yet full, so God waited for the right time.

• **God's justice with His own people**
	+ God dealt with His own people, Israel, in the same justice as with the Canaanites.
	+ Israel sinned against God and walked after the fashions of the heathen.

• **The consequences of sin**
	+ Sin can overturn kingdoms, regardless of their power or strength.
	+ The sins of a people are like worms and cankers, eating out the life and strength of a commonwealth.

• **The importance of looking to our lives**
	+ We should look to our lives and make conscience of all sins, especially great and capital ones.

	+ Civil or politic reasons: Abraham was a servant of God and had to move frequently.
	+ Holy or religious reasons: Abraham and other holy patriarchs wanted to testify their faith and expectation of a better world.

	+ Abraham and other holy men had to move frequently, following God's call.
	+ Tents were the most practical and cheapest option for their nomadic lifestyle.

• **Holy reasons for dwelling in tents**
	+ Abraham and other holy men saw themselves as strangers on earth.
	+ They wanted to testify their faith and expectation of a better world by dwelling in tents.

• **Tents as a symbol of faith**
	+ Tents represented a desire to remove from the earth and seek a heavenly home.
	+ This was a way for holy men to testify their faith and expectation of a better world.

• **Other holy men who dwelt in tents**
	+ Noah, the king of the world, dwelt in tents.
	+ Lot, Abraham's nephew, also dwelt in tents.

• **The apostle's perspective**
	+ The apostle says that holy men in those days had no enduring city, but sought one to come (Heb. 13:14).

• **Conclusion**
	+ Abraham's decision to dwell in tents was a deliberate choice, motivated by his faith and expectation of a better world.

	+ God's Word teaches that heaven is not the place of His substance and being
	+ The Godhead is not comprehended or contained in any place

	+ We don't know the day it was created, but that doesn't mean it didn't exist
	+ The same is true of the angels, who are also invisible

	+ It was made by God, and therefore is a creature

	+ It shows that God is a creator who makes things for His glory and the benefit of His creatures
	+ It also shows that God's presence is not limited to a physical place, but can be manifest in various ways.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The object of true faith**
	+ The main promise is the promise of salvation by Christ (John 3:16)
	+ True faith directly and plainly fastens itself on this promise
	+ After and with this, true faith fastens itself on all other promises that concern soul or body

• **The nature of true faith**
	+ True faith is free and voluntary
	+ True faith is not constrained, but is based on a good foundation
	+ True faith is persuaded that God accepts us in Christ for salvation

• **The scope of true faith**
	+ True faith apprehends not only the promise of redemption by Christ, but all other inferior promises that depend on it
	+ True faith takes hold of the promise of a temporal blessing by the same faith that justifies us

• **Abraham and Sarah's faith**
	+ Abraham and Sarah believed the promise of a child by the same faith that justified them
	+ This shows that true faith apprehends all promises that depend on the main promise of salvation by Christ

• **The impediments of Sarah's faith**
	+ Sarah was past age (90 years old) and it was unlikely that she could bear a child
	+ Sarah was also barren, which made it even less likely that she could conceive

• **The objection to Sarah's age**
	+ In the old time, people had children till they were of great age (e.g. Eve had Seth at 330 years old)
	+ However, this was before the flood, when people lived much longer (8-900 years)

• **The reality of Sarah's situation**
	+ Sarah lived after the flood, when ages were brought down to 200 years or less
	+ Abraham lived 175 years, and Sarah lived 127 years, making her past age for childbearing at 90 years old

• **Sarah's response to the promise**
	+ Despite the impediments of her age and barrenness, Sarah believed the promise of God
	+ She rested and relied only on God's word, beyond all reason and impediments

• **The nature of faith in the face of obstacles**
	+ True faith is not constrained by obstacles or impediments
	+ True faith is free and voluntary, and is based on a good foundation

• **The importance of faith in prayer**
	+ We are bound to believe that God will give us temporal blessings, such as health, peace, and competency of wealth, if we ask in faith
	+ We are to pray for these things under the name of daily bread, and believe that God will provide them for us.

• **Sarah's Conception**

	+ The law and power to increase and multiply is given to both humans and beasts (Gen. 1:22)

	+ Making children know God makes them fellow citizens with the angels
	+ This would bring great blessings to the church and commonwealth

	+ Sarah figures the spiritual Jerusalem, the church of God
	+ This is a sound and sure allegory, as seen in the apostles' writings (Gal. 4:23, etc.)

	+ Men are regenerated and born to the church through the power of God's word and Spirit

	+ We should honor the church as our mother, but worship God alone as the Father of our soul
	+ The church cannot make herself our mother or us her children, but it is God who must speak the word

	+ God's holy word is the immortal seed of our regeneration
	+ We are made God's children and heirs of immortality through the power of His word

• **The Ground of Sarah's Faith**
	+ The foundation of Sarah's faith was not just the bare promise of God, but her conceit or opinion of Him
	+ Her faith was built on her understanding of God's character and power

	+ Not just the value of the promise itself

	+ This is the foundation of our faith in God and His promises

• **Two essential qualities of God**
	+ **Ability**: God has all-sufficient power to make good on His promises
	+ **Carefulness**: God is willing and careful to do what He has said

	+ Some people promise more than they can deliver
	+ Others are able to perform, but lack care and follow through on their promises

	+ God has both the ability and care to fulfill His promises
	+ This is why we can trust in His word and promises
