Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Holy Ghost uses comparisons to express the multitude of the Israelites**
	+ Two comparisons are used: as many as the stars in the sky and as many as the sands by the seashore
	+ These comparisons are used to convey the idea of an incredible number of people

• **The comparisons are taken from the heavens and the earth**
	+ The stars in the sky are used to convey the idea of a vast and infinite number
	+ The sands by the seashore are used to convey the idea of a number that is too great to count

• **Other comparisons could have been used, but these two are most common and proverbial**
	+ The drops of water, dust of the earth, and hairs of men's heads could also be used to convey the idea of a multitude
	+ However, the stars and sands are more commonly used in everyday language

• **The comparison of the stars was first used by God with Abraham**
	+ God told Abraham to count the stars, and said that his seed would be as numerous as they
	+ Moses later used the same comparison in Deuteronomy 10:22

• **The comparison of the sands was used by Moses to convey the idea of a multitude**
	+ Moses said that the Israelites were as numerous as the sands by the seashore
	+ This comparison is used to convey the idea that the number of Israelites is too great to count

• **The Holy Ghost uses figurative language to convey the idea of a multitude**
	+ The comparisons of the stars and sands are not meant to be taken literally
	+ They are used to convey the idea of a number that is too great to count

• **The Holy Ghost uses rhetorical devices to convey the idea of a multitude**
	+ The comparisons of the stars and sands are examples of rhetorical devices used in the Bible
	+ Rhetoric is a good and lawful art, as it is used by the Holy Ghost to convey the idea of a multitude

• **The comparison of the sands is a figure of speech that uses the idea of a lip to convey the idea of a boundary**
	+ The word "lip" is used to describe the shore of the sea, which is a figure of speech
	+ The shore of the sea is compared to a lip, which encloses the mouth

• **The Holy Ghost uses rhetorical devices to convey the idea of a multitude in other places in the Bible**
	+ The Bible uses other rhetorical devices, such as hyperbole and metaphor, to convey the idea of a multitude
	+ Examples of this can be seen in John 21:25 and Deuteronomy 9:1

• **The use of rhetorical devices in the Bible is a warrantable and good art**
	+ The Holy Ghost uses rhetorical devices to convey the idea of a multitude, and therefore it is a good and lawful art.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Scripture's use of rhetoric**
	+ The Bible, including many of Paul's epistles and Christ's sermons, uses elegant rhetoric.
	+ Prophets like Isaiah also use rhetorical devices.
	+ This suggests that using rhetoric is not only permissible but also a common practice in Scripture.

• **Collecting and teaching rhetoric**
	+ If it's lawful to practice rhetoric, it's also lawful to collect and teach its rules.
	+ Those who claim it's unlawful to teach rhetoric are going against the common practice of Scripture and reason.

• **Preaching and eloquence**
	+ It's lawful and warrantable for ministers to use rhetoric and eloquence in preaching.
	+ This is because the Holy Spirit uses rhetoric in Scripture, and ministers can use similar techniques.

• **Limitations on eloquence**
	+ Not all kinds of eloquence are permitted to Christian ministers.
	+ Ministers must use a "holy, sanctified, and spiritual eloquence" that's fit for spiritual things.

• **Purifying human eloquence**
	+ Human eloquence must be "brought home to divinity" and purified with spiritual wisdom.
	+ This means that human eloquence must be adapted to convey spiritual truths.

• **Cautions for using eloquence**
	+ The more natural and less affected the eloquence is, the more commendable it is.
	+ Eloquence should be grave, sober, and modest, remembering the importance of the task.

• **Avoiding distractions**
	+ Eloquence should not be used to tell strange tales or use gestures that might distract or amuse the audience.
	+ Wit and humor can be used, but they should not be the primary focus.

• **Supporting understanding**
	+ Eloquence should be used to help, not hinder, the understanding of God's word.
	+ God's word should not be adapted to fit the eloquence, but rather the eloquence should be used to convey the word.

• **Expressing meaning clearly**
	+ Eloquence should be used to express the meaning of God's word clearly, purely, and plainly.
	+ This means avoiding ambiguity and using language that's easily understood by the audience.

• **Qualifications for using eloquence**
	+ Eloquence should be used with qualifications, such as avoiding distractions and supporting understanding.
	+ With these qualifications, eloquence can be a valuable tool for conveying spiritual truths.

* **The Patriarchs' Joy and Acceptance of God's Promises**

	+ They took the promise kindly by practicing their faith in life
	+ They built altars and offered sacrifices to testify their joy and acceptance
* **The Same Promises Made to Us**
	+ The Lord has made the same promises to us as to Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob
	+ We have the same promises already accomplished and verified
	+ We should take the promises more thankfully and kindly than the patriarchs did
* **Our Unkindness in Regard to God's Promises**
	+ We are an unkind people in regard to the mercies and promises of God
	+ We are not as joyful to hear the gospel as the patriarchs were
	+ We are hardhearted and unkind, and insensible to God's great favors and mercies
* **Our Behavior in God's Worship**
	+ We worship God formally, not in a way of thankfulness
	+ We come to worship God out of habit or custom, not out of a willing heart
	+ We do not regard the preached or read word, thinking it is not essential to worship
* **The Difference Between Our Worship and the Patriarchs'**
	+ The patriarchs built altars and offered sacrifices to signify their kindness and willing heart
	+ We worship God with little more than a lip-labor, without faith in our hearts
	+ We are degenerate from the faith of our forefathers, the holy patriarchs

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **First Fruit of the Patriarchs' Faith**
	+ They received God's promises kindly and thankfully
	+ This is evident in their obedience to God's commands

• **Second Fruit of the Patriarchs' Faith**

	+ We should strive to obey God's commands and profess our faith openly
	+ We should not be ashamed of our holy profession of Christianity, but rather follow the example of the patriarchs.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Christians are strangers and pilgrims**
	+ They dwell in a strange country, not their own
	+ They are on a journey to their true home, heaven

• **Patriarchs were strangers and pilgrims**
	+ Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob dwelt as strangers in tents
	+ They were always ready to go wherever God called them

• **Christians must follow the patriarchs' example**
	+ They must testify and profess themselves as strangers and pilgrims

• **How to be answerable to this profession**
	+ Use the world and its things as if they were not yours
	+ Do not set your heart on temporal blessings

• **Using the world's things as strangers**
	+ Use your house as a stranger uses an inn
	+ Use your goods as a pilgrim uses another's goods for a night

• **Casting off hindrances to the kingdom of heaven**
	+ Cast off all things that may hinder you in your journey
	+ Sin is the greatest hindrance to the kingdom of heaven

• **Sin is a burden that hinders our journey**
	+ Sin presses us down and holds us back
	+ We must take heed of all sin to be good travelers and pilgrims

• **The way to the kingdom of heaven is narrow**
	+ The gate is narrow and few can enter
	+ We must come with a humble and pure heart to enter the gate

• **Characteristics of those who cannot enter the gate**
	+ The proud, covetous, ambitious, and luxurious cannot enter
	+ These characteristics are like weights that hold us back

• **Characteristics of those who can enter the gate**
	+ The meek in spirit, who lead a humble and innocent life, can enter
	+ These characteristics are like light weights that allow us to move forward

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **To become a good pilgrim, we must:**
	+ Cast off every sin through true repentance
	+ Make ourselves fit for the way to heaven
	+ Learn contentation of heart in every estate of life

• **Contentation of heart means:**
	+ Being patient in sickness and health
	+ Being content in poverty and plenty
	+ Being patient in trouble and peace
	+ Being content in good report and ill report

• **A pilgrim's behavior:**
	+ Takes all things patiently that befall him
	+ Does not seek revenge or make complaint
	+ Waits for redress and rest in heaven

• **The world is taken in two ways:**
	+ For corruptions and sins in the world (to be contemned)
	+ For temporal blessings (not to be contemned)

• **Temporal blessings are:**
	+ Money, lands, wealth, sustenance, and other outward things
	+ Good gifts of God, not to be simply contemned

• **Voluntary poverty is not a good state of life:**
	+ It is a mere device of man's brain
	+ Has no warrant in God's Word
	+ Is not a state of perfection or near to the state of glory

• **The command to sell all was special:**
	+ Given to the young rich man in Mark 10
	+ A command of trial, not ordinary
	+ Meant to show his corruption and hypocrisy

• **Christ's command was not to give all:**
	+ Only to sell all and give to the poor
	+ Not a command to give all

• **Christ and His disciples were not beggars:**
	+ The bag carried by Judas proves the contrary
	+ Christ's disciples had title and possession in their houses

• **The example of Christ and His disciples:**
	+ Does not support the idea of voluntary poverty
	+ Is a mere forgery and cannot be proved out of the Word of God

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Question 1: Seeking wealth as a pilgrim**
	+ The question is whether a person can lawfully seek to be rich while professing to be a pilgrim and stranger in this life.
	+ The answer is that it depends on how one defines "riches".

• **Defining "riches"**
	+ "Riches" can be taken two ways: as things sufficient for a person's estate, or as abundance above what is necessary.
	+ The first definition is lawful, while the second is not.

• **Seeking things necessary for life**
	+ A person can lawfully seek to be rich in the sense of having things necessary for life, as taught in the Lord's Prayer.
	+ This includes asking God for daily bread and using means to attain necessary things.

• **The importance of contentment**
	+ A person's corrupt nature makes them covetous and discontent with what they have.
	+ To overcome this, one must learn to be content with what is sufficient and follow the example of wise and godly men.

• **Apparel and personal adornment**
	+ There is no special rule or precept in God's Word regarding the wearing of apparel.
	+ One's direction should be the example of the most grave and godly in their calling.

• **The dangers of seeking abundance**
	+ Seeking abundance above what is necessary is not lawful, as it can lead to temptation and destruction.
	+ This is condemned by the apostle Paul in 1 Timothy 6:9.

• **The sin of covetousness**
	+ Many people are covetous and will not be content with what is enough, but instead toil and moil for more.
	+ This is condemned by the Holy Ghost.

• **What to do with abundance**
	+ If God gives a person abundance by lawful means, they must think themselves appointed as a steward over it.
	+ They must use the abundance for the glory of God and the good of His church.

• **The importance of behaving like pilgrims**
	+ The practice of the patriarchs is necessary for us today, as we have not behaved ourselves like pilgrims and strangers in this world.
	+ We must learn to use the things of this life as though we used them not, so as they do not hinder the growth of God's graces in us.

• **The consequences of entangling ourselves with worldly things**
	+ If we entangle ourselves with the things of this life, we will choke the word of God and prevent it from taking root in our hearts.
	+ We must shake off this sin and learn to behave like pilgrims and strangers.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **The Holy Spirit's Reason for the Patriarchs' Perseverance**
	+ The Holy Spirit is proving that Abraham, Sarah, Isaac, and Jacob were constant in their faith until the end.
	+ The reason is that they sought a country, which was heaven.

* **The Patriarchs' Desire for Heaven**
	+ They sought for their country, which was heaven.
	+ This desire was not just a sudden motion, but a constant persuasion and settled resolution.

* **Counterarguments**
	+ Some may think that seeking heaven is not a sufficient argument for true faith.
	+ They may point to Balaam, who desired to die like the righteous, but did not live like them.

* **The Difference between Balaam and the Patriarchs**
	+ Balaam's desire was not grounded on a constant persuasion or settled resolution.
	+ He would not live the life of the righteous, and had no delight in walking in their way.

* **The Importance of Timing**
	+ Many will seek to come to heaven, but will not be able to enter because they seek when the door of mercy is shut.
	+ The patriarchs, however, sought heaven in due time, not for a brief moment.

* **The Patriarchs' Actions**
	+ They went the right way, denying themselves and their estate in this life.
	+ They were willing to forsake all things in this world to attain heaven.

* **The Holy Spirit's Division of the Reason**
	+ The Holy Spirit divides the reason into two parts: that the patriarchs sought a country, and that this country was heaven itself.

* **The First Part of the Reason**
	+ The patriarchs said they were pilgrims and strangers, which shows that they sought a country.
	+ This is evident, as anyone who says they are a pilgrim and stranger is seeking a new country.

* **The Second Part of the Reason**
	+ The patriarchs' confession in the former verse is assumed to be true.
	+ Therefore, they sought a country, which is heaven.

* **The Author's Knowledge of the Patriarchs' History**
	+ The author of the epistle had diligently read the history of the patriarchs in the book of Genesis.
	+ He observed their confession of being pilgrims and strangers, and gathered from it that they sought a heavenly country.

	+ This shows that they were not mindful of their earthly country, but instead desired a heavenly one

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **The Importance of Choosing the Best**
	+ The author observes that people often choose the lesser of two options
	+ This is seen in the way people speak of past events as if they were present
* **The Patriarchs' Choice**
	+ The patriarchs were given a choice between the land of Canaan and the kingdom of heaven
	+ They chose the kingdom of heaven, which was not yet present, over the land of Canaan, which was present
* **David's Choice**
	+ David chose to be a doorkeeper in God's house rather than living with the wicked
	+ He preferred a life of service to God over a life of ease and comfort with the wicked
* **Solomon's Choice**
	+ Solomon chose a wise and understanding heart over riches and honor
	+ He was highly commended by God for making this choice
* **Moses' Choice**
	+ Moses chose to suffer adversity with the people of God rather than living a life of ease and comfort in Pharaoh's court
	+ He refused to be called the son of Pharaoh's daughter and instead chose to identify with the people of God
* **Esau's Choice**
	+ Esau chose the worse option, his birthright, over a bowl of red broth
	+ He was marked as profane by God for making this choice
* **The Devil's Choice**
	+ The devil sets before people vain pleasures and delights
	+ He shows them the profits and pleasures of sin, but not the consequences
* **The Human Choice**
	+ Most people prefer the vanities and pleasures of the sinful world over Christ and salvation
	+ They make no account of God's blessings and instead choose sin and its companions
* **The Need for Spiritual Wisdom**
	+ People need to pray to God for spiritual wisdom and the gift of discerning
	+ They need to be able to discern between good and evil and choose the best option
* **The Consequences of a Bad Choice**
	+ Those who choose sin over God's blessings will suffer the consequences
	+ They will live a life of bitterness and regret, and will never be able to change their choice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Heavenly Country**
	+ The patriarchs desired a better country than the land of Canaan, which was a heavenly country, even heaven itself.
	+ This is the last and chief point in the reason to wit, and the proof is intended in this place.

• **Following the Patriarchs' Example**
	+ As our forefathers in faith and patterns, the patriarchs' example teaches us to aim at a better country than the one we live in.
	+ We must all seek the kingdom of heaven, not thinking that this world is the country we are born for.

• **The Duty to Seek Heaven**
	+ We must seek heaven, regardless of our age, social status, or education.
	+ We must do this not only at death, but also in the time of our youth and strength.

• **Reasons to Seek Heaven**
	+ Worldly wisdom teaches us to be prepared for the unexpected, just as we would if we were living in someone else's house.
	+ Our bodies are temporary tents and tabernacles, and time is uncertain.

• **The State of the World**
	+ Sin, atheism, and profaneness abound everywhere, and God's judgments are often inflicted upon us.
	+ We do not know how God will deal with us for our sins, and we must provide for ourselves a resting place.

• **The Consequences of Not Seeking Heaven**
	+ If we do not seek heaven, we will be due for eternal woe and destruction, the bottomless pit of hell.
	+ Sin and Satan will deceive us, persuading us that we can come to heaven while continuing in our corrupt nature.

• **The Gift of Faith in Christ**
	+ Through the gift of faith in Christ, we can come to the heavenly city that the patriarchs so seriously sought for.
	+ We must labor to eschew the place of destruction and come to the heavenly city.

• **The Importance of Using Good Means**
	+ It stands us in hand to use all good means to come to heaven, or else our case will be the most miserable of all creatures.
	+ Perdition and destruction will be our portion if we do not seek heaven.

• **Awakening Our Dead and Drowsy Hearts**
	+ Our dead and drowsy hearts must be awakened and stirred up to seek heaven.
	+ We must take care and pains to do something for our own everlasting good.

• **The Contrast between Heaven and Hell**
	+ Heaven is a blessed country where we may abide forever in all blessedness.
	+ Hell is a place of eternal woe and destruction, the bottomless pit of eternal torment.

• **God's Favor towards the Patriarchs**

	+ It means God vouchsafed unto them this favor and showed them this honor

	+ God accepted them in His mercy to be part of His covenant of salvation

	+ It is the way to all true honor, as it has done in this world for all who have tried it
	+ It is the foundation for true honor among men.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **God's Love and Favor**
	+ God was not ashamed to be called the God of the Israelites
	+ He prepared a city for them, showing His love and favor
	+ This proves that God delights in them rather than being ashamed of them
* **The Importance of Having God as Your God**
	+ Having God as your God means having all things with Him
	+ Lack of God means lack of all things
	+ David says, "Blessed are the people whose God is the Lord" (Ps. 145:15)
* **Abraham's Faith**
	+ Abraham's faith is commended for offering up Isaac, his only begotten son
	+ This act of faith is notable and unmatchable
* **The Work of Abraham's Faith**
	+ The work of Abraham's faith is laid down as offering up Isaac
	+ This work is commended by three special arguments:
		- Three great impediments that might have hindered this work of faith
		- Abraham's victory over these impediments
		- The issue of this temptation and the work of faith therein
* **The Impediments to Abraham's Faith**
	+ The law of nature and the law of God prohibit killing one's own son
	+ Abraham had a special commandment to kill Isaac, which he obeyed by faith
* **The Conflict between Two Commandments**
	+ The commandment "Thou shalt not kill" seems to conflict with the special commandment to kill Isaac
	+ However, one commandment must give place to the other when they cross each other
* **The Rule of Precedence**
	+ When two commandments of God cross each other, a man must prefer the greater
	+ The main duties of the first table (e.g. honoring God) take precedence over the main duties of the second table (e.g. honoring parents and magistrates)
* **The Example of Christ**
	+ Christ says, "If any man come to me, and hate not his father and mother, wife and children, he cannot be my disciple" (Luke 14:26)
	+ This shows that the main duties of the first table take precedence over the main duties of the second table
* **The Significance of Abraham's Faith**
	+ Abraham's faith is an example of how to obey God's commandments, even when they seem to conflict with each other
	+ His faith is notable and unmatchable, and it is commended by the Holy Ghost.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Prioritizing God's will over human relationships**
	+ If following God's commandments means disobeying human relationships, we must prioritize God's will.
	+ This means we may have to "hate" our loved ones in order to obey God.

• **Ceremonial law vs. law of charity and love**
	+ Ceremonial laws (e.g. Sabbath rest) can be overridden by laws of charity and love (e.g. helping a neighbor in need).
	+ Charity and love are considered more important than ceremonial laws.

• **Example: Sabbath rest vs. helping a neighbor**
	+ If a neighbor's house is on fire on the Sabbath, we may labor to help them, even if it means breaking the Sabbath rest.
	+ This is because helping a neighbor is a work of mercy and a main duty of the second table.

• **God's special commandments take precedence**
	+ If God gives a special commandment that contradicts one of the 10 commandments, we must follow the special commandment.
	+ This means that ordinary commandments can be overridden by special commandments.

• **Abraham's example: sacrificing Isaac**
	+ God gave Abraham a special commandment to sacrifice Isaac, which would normally be considered a heinous sin.
	+ Abraham was warranted to sacrifice Isaac because of the special commandment.

• **The importance of obedience to God's will**
	+ God is an absolute lord and is not bound by His own laws.
	+ He can dispense with them and with us for the keeping of them at His will and pleasure.

• **The danger of imitating God's actions without understanding**
	+ The Jews were condemned for burning their sons and daughters in sacrifice, even though they thought they were imitating Abraham.
	+ This shows the importance of understanding God's will and not just imitating His actions.

• **Jephthah's example: sacrificing his daughter**
	+ Some think Jephthah sacrificed his daughter in sacrifice to the Lord, but this is not necessarily true.
	+ We should be cautious in our interpretations and not jump to conclusions.

• **Abraham's faith and obedience**
	+ Abraham had faith and obedience to God's will, even when it seemed to contradict the ordinary commandments.
	+ This faith and obedience were justified by God's special commandment.

• **God's perspective vs. human perspective**
	+ God's perspective is what matters, not human understanding or interpretation.
	+ In God's sight, Abraham's actions were justified, even if they seemed wrong to humans.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God accepts the will for the deed**
	+ God values a person's intention and effort over their actual accomplishments.
	+ This is seen in the example of the poor widow who gave two mites, which was more valuable to God than the large amounts given by others.

• **Obedience is key**
	+ A person's endeavor to obey God is what matters, not their success or failure.
	+ God will accept and be pleased with a person's efforts, even if they fail many times.

• **This doctrine is comforting**
	+ It brings comfort to a distressed conscience, knowing that God values intention over accomplishment.
	+ However, it must not be used as an excuse to sin.

• **Abuse of this doctrine**
	+ Some people use this doctrine to justify living in sin, claiming that God will accept them because they love Him in their heart.
	+ However, this is a false interpretation, and God will not be merciful to those who flatter themselves in their sins.

• **Abraham's obedience**

	+ We should be like good servants and obey our Lord and Master
	+ This is essential for being Abraham's children

• **The Time of God's Intervention**
	+ God often lets His servants alone for a long season in temptation and distress
	+ He shows Himself and brings comfort to them when they're in extremity
	+ This is seen in Abraham's experience and the church's search for Christ

• **The Joyous and Comfortable Issue**
	+ When we cast ourselves on God's providence, we'll have a joyous and comfortable issue
	+ This is seen in Abraham's experience and the church's search for Christ
	+ It's a promise for all who fear the Lord and trust in His good pleasure.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Israelites' Experience**
	+ They were in bondage in Egypt for many years
	+ Moses was sent by God to deliver them
	+ They cried out to Moses when they were trapped between the Red Sea and Pharaoh's army
	+ God parted the Red Sea, allowing them to pass through safely
	+ Pharaoh's army was drowned in the Red Sea

	+ God often waits until we are in a desperate situation before helping us

	+ This is a test of our faith and a way for God to manifest His graces in us

	+ Abraham was asked to sacrifice his son Isaac as a test of his faith
	+ Instead of Isaac, Abraham offered a ram that was caught in a bush
	+ This event teaches us that our sacrifices to God must be living sacrifices
	+ We must offer ourselves to God, not dead in sin, but living in righteousness and holiness

	+ We must consecrate ourselves to God's service and obey Him in our lives
	+ We must renounce ourselves and be dedicated to God alone
	+ Sin makes our sacrifice dead, lame, halt, and blind, which God abhors
	+ We must bring ourselves to God as living sacrifices, holy and acceptable to Him

	+ Paul teaches us to give up our bodies as living sacrifices to God
	+ This is our reasonable service to God, as He has shown us mercy and love

	+ However, this is not the focus of the text, and the author does not develop this idea further

• **Isaac's Faith**
	+ The Holy Spirit commends Isaac's faith in this verse
	+ We are to mark four points about Isaac's faith: the blessing, the cause of the blessing, the parties blessed, and the nature of the blessing

• **The Blessing**
	+ Isaac blessed Jacob and Esau concerning things to come
	+ This blessing is a key aspect of Isaac's faith

• **The Cause of the Blessing**
	+ The cause of the blessing is Isaac's faith
	+ His faith is the foundation of the blessing he gives to Jacob and Esau

• **The Parties Blessed**
	+ The parties blessed are Jacob and Esau
	+ This blessing has implications for their future and their relationship with God

• **The Nature of the Blessing**
	+ The nature of the blessing is that it concerns things to come
	+ This blessing looks to the future and has a prophetic element to it

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Three kinds of blessings**
	+ God blesses man
	+ Man blesses God
	+ One man blesses another

• **God blesses man**
	+ Gives gifts and benefits (temporal or spiritual)
	+ Blessed all creatures in the beginning, especially man

• **Man blesses God**
	+ Praises God and gives thanks
	+ Example: Paul's blessing in Ephesians 1:3
	+ Example: Zechariah's song of praise in Luke 1:68

• **One man blesses another**
	+ Private blessing: one person prays for a blessing on another
	+ Public blessing: done by a minister in the name of God

• **Private blessing**
	+ Common to all men
	+ Especially belongs to parents blessing their children
	+ Example: Parents praying for their children's well-being

• **Public blessing**
	+ Done by a minister in the name of God
	+ Example: Melchizedek blessing Abraham in Hebrews 7:1

• **Types of public blessings**
	+ Ordinary: pronounced and uttered from God's Word by an ordinary minister
	+ Extraordinary: not specified in the text

• **Ordinary public blessing**
	+ Example: Priests in the Old Testament blessing the people
	+ Form of blessing prescribed by Moses in Numbers 6:23

• **Christ's blessing**
	+ Performed to His disciples at His ascension
	+ Example: Christ giving gifts to men in Ephesians 4:8-12

• **Christ's blessing as mediator**
	+ Signified the blessings Christ would give to the church in the New Testament
	+ Christ's blessing is not only a pronouncement, but also a gift

• **The Nature of Blessing**

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Devil's Influence**
	+ Men who seek out wizards and wise women are seeking help from the devil.

	+ They are using the devil's "laws and rites" (words, spells, and charms) to bless men's goods and children.

	+ The Lord warns against seeking help from those who work with spirits and soothsayers.
	+ Those who do so will be cut off from God's people (Lev. 20:6).
	+ The Lord will set His face against them and cut them off.

• **The Danger of Blessers**
	+ These individuals seem harmless, but they are more dangerous than notorious witches.
	+ They ensnare the soul and draw whole towns and countries to their wickedness.
	+ They have no fear of God and set themselves against God's Word.

• **The Order of Blessing**
	+ The Holy Ghost uses a threefold order in naming persons: nature, dignity, and history.
	+ In the case of Jacob and Esau, the order is one of dignity, not nature or history.

• **The Reason for Jacob's Priority**
	+ Jacob is put first because he is chief in the favor of God.
	+ The Holy Ghost uses this order of dignity to show that Jacob is the one who receives the principal blessing.

• **The Blessing of Life Everlasting**
	+ The first blessing (of life everlasting) belongs to Jacob.
	+ Esau must have his blessing, but in the second place.

• **The Cause of Jacob's Blessing**
	+ The good pleasure of God is the chief cause of Jacob's blessing.
	+ It is not because of Jacob's desert or Esau's default.

• **Esau's Situation**
	+ Esau is put by and loses the principal blessing.
	+ He is not rejected because of his default, but because of the good pleasure of God.

• **Jacob's Actions**
	+ Jacob gets a kid dressed and comes to his father with it.
	+ He talks with his father while glossing over his actions.

• **The Importance of God's Word**
	+ The Lord's Word is clear: do not seek help from those who work with spirits and soothsayers.
	+ We must fear the curse of God and not seek help from those who have no fear of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's love is not based on human behavior**
	+ God's special love for a person is not grounded on their behavior, but on His own good will and pleasure.
	+ This is seen in the blessing of Jacob, who did not deserve it, but received it anyway.

• **God's counsel overrules human will**
	+ God's counsel overruled Isaac's will, causing him to bless Jacob instead of Esau.
	+ This shows that God's will is the highest and chiefest cause of all, and it always comes to pass.

• **God's will is not dependent on human cooperation**
	+ The idea that God wills to save all men, but they are not saved because they will not be saved, is erroneous.
	+ This idea makes human will overrule God's counsel, which is not the case.

• **Jacob's blessing was not deserved**
	+ Jacob came dissemblingly and lying to his father, but he was still blessed.
	+ This shows that God's blessing is not based on human merit, but on His own good pleasure.

	+ Jacob should have sought the blessing in a more honest and humble way.
	+ However, God still chose to bless him, despite his wrong approach.

	+ When we seek God's blessing, we must not come in our own "garments" of righteousness.
	+ Instead, we must put on Christ's garment, the long, white robe of His righteousness.

	+ When we put on Christ's righteousness by faith, it is a sweet-smelling savor to God.
	+ This is seen in the blessing of Jacob, who was accepted by God because of his faith in God's promise.

	+ We must seek to resemble Christ in knowledge, righteousness, and holiness.
	+ This is the only way to increase our assurance of God's blessings.

• **God's blessing is irreversible**
	+ Once God has pronounced a blessing, it must stand.
	+ This is seen in the blessing of Jacob, which Isaac pronounced despite his initial intention to bless Esau.

• **Ministers of God's Word have the power to pronounce God's blessing**
	+ Even weak and fallible men can pronounce God's blessing on the people.
	+ However, this blessing is not dependent on human merit, but on God's good pleasure.

**1. Jacob's Humility and Faith**

	* The prophecy that the elder should serve the younger referred to their posterity

	* David's words in Psalm 60:8 show that he would bring the Edomites into a low estate

	* Isaac blessed Esau, saying he would have a fertile soil and the fatness of the earth
	* However, Malachi says God hated Esau and made his mountains waste
	* The answer is that the land of Edom was fertile in Isaac's time, but became barren later

	* The text discusses the faith of Jacob and others, including healers and diviners
	* Perkins is referring to the proto-Arminian strain within the English church

**5. Jacob's Blessing of Ephraim and Manasseh**
	* Jacob blessed the two sons of Joseph when he was dying
	* He worshipped on the end of his staff, showing his faith and adoration of God

**6. The Blessing Itself**
	* The blessing was extraordinary and prophetic, not just a common fatherly blessing
	* It consisted of three things:
		+ Adopting Ephraim and Manasseh as his own sons
		+ Giving them two portions in the land of Canaan
		+ Foretelling their future greatness and the two great people that would come from them

**7. The Circumstances of the Blessing**
	* The blessing took place when Jacob was dying
	* He blessed them by faith, not just by natural means

**8. The Parties Blessed**
	* Ephraim and Manasseh were the two sons of Joseph
	* They were blessed by Jacob, who adopted them as his own sons

**9. The Significance of the Blessing**
	* The blessing shows Jacob's faith and prophetic gift
	* It also shows God's plan for the future of Ephraim and Manasseh

**10. Lessons from Jacob's Blessing**
	* We learn about the importance of faith and prophetic gifts
	* We also learn about the significance of adoption and the blessing of God on individuals and nations.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's Liberty in Adoption**
	+ God allows a master of a family to adopt and choose a child to uphold their household
	+ This is seen in Jacob's adoption of Joseph's sons, Ephraim and Manasseh
	+ The master must ensure that adoption does not unjustly hinder their own issue or kindred

• **God's Revelation to His Children**
	+ God reveals His will and counsel to His children in a special manner
	+ This is seen in Jacob's blessing of Ephraim and Manasseh, where God reveals their future estate
	+ God also reveals His secrets to His servants, the prophets, and to those who are His friends

• **Friendship with God**
	+ Those who are friends of God will know things that God will not reveal to others
	+ This is seen in Jesus' statement to His disciples, "I have revealed unto you all that I have heard of my Father" (John 15:15)
	+ God's friends will have a special knowledge of their own election, justification, sanctification, and glorification

• **The Time of Blessing**
	+ The time of blessing is when Jacob was dying, but not in the act of dying
	+ This teaches two special duties: one for masters of families and one for ministers of God's Word

• **Masters of Families**
	+ Masters of families are taught to set their houses in order before they die
	+ This involves disposing of their temporal things and distributing their inheritance
	+ They must also give charge of duties concerning themselves and others, especially concerning religion and God's worship

• **Ministers of God's Word**
	+ Ministers of God's Word are taught to bear the person of a prophet, like Jacob
	+ They must set their own houses in order and give charge of duties concerning themselves and others

• **Setting the House in Order**
	+ Masters of families must set their houses in order by two duties:
		- Disposing of their temporal goods and possessions
		- Giving exhortation and charge to their children and family concerning the worship of God and true religion

• **King David's Example**
	+ King David set his house in order by giving charge to Solomon, his son, and making him king in his stead
	+ He also gave him exhortation and charge concerning the worship of God and true religion

• **The Importance of Setting the House in Order**
	+ Setting the house in order is essential for masters of families and ministers of God's Word
	+ It involves disposing of temporal things, giving charge of duties, and ensuring the continuation of true religion

• **The Consequences of Not Setting the House in Order**
	+ Not setting the house in order can lead to chaos and disorder in the family and community
	+ It can also hinder the continuation of true religion and the worship of God

	+ Jacob's example shows us the importance of worshipping God, even in our old age and weakness.

	+ To die well, praising God, we must lead our lives by faith and the direction of God's Word and promises.
	+ This will enable us to withstand temptation and sickness at the end of our lives.

	+ Sound zeal will never decay or weaken with the body.

	+ Jacob's sound zeal was evident at his death, despite his physical weakness.
	+ This shows that true zeal will flourish in old age, even if bodily strength decays.
* **Getting Sound Zeal**
	+ To show forth zeal in our age, we must get soundness of it in our youth.
	+ This will put forth itself in the time of death.
* **Joseph's Faith**
	+ Joseph's faith is an example of the faith of the patriarchs.

	+ The pleasures of sin refer to the riches and dignity that Moses could have had in Pharaoh's court
	+ These pleasures are not inherently good, but rather a means to sin and live apart from God's people

• **Reasons for Moses' refusal**
	+ Moses refused the pleasures of sin because they were a means to sin
	+ He also refused them because they would only be enjoyed for a season, not providing lasting happiness

• **Riches and honor**
	+ Riches, honor, and dignity are nothing but the pleasures and profits of sin when severed from true religion
	+ They are mere vanity and vexation of spirit, as Solomon and Paul have testified

• **The state of men who prioritize wealth**
	+ Men who prioritize wealth and preferment over religion and good conscience are miserable
	+ They are considered wise by the world, but their case is actually one of great woe

• **The dangers of worldly treasures**
	+ Worldly treasures severed from religion are the mammon of iniquity, which causes damnation
	+ Christ taught that it is difficult for a rich man to enter the kingdom of heaven, as they are often consumed by their wealth

• **The fate of the wealthy**
	+ The wealthy who prioritize their riches over Christ and His gospel will receive a greater condemnation
	+ They will be wretched and miserable before God, as they have received their consolation in this life

• **The importance of true religion**
	+ True religion is essential for happiness and salvation
	+ Without it, riches and honors are mere vanity and a means to sin

• **Moses' example**
	+ Moses' choice to suffer with God's people rather than enjoying the pleasures of sin is an example for us to follow
	+ We should prioritize our relationship with God and His people over worldly wealth and honors.

	+ To persuade us to suffer with joy, we must consider the reward.

• **The nature of reward**
	+ Everlasting life is called a reward, but it is not earned by works.
	+ It is a free gift of God, promised to His children in Christ.

• **Why Christ cannot merit**
	+ As a man, Christ could not merit anything at God's hands.
	+ He did not do works of himself, but from the fullness of the Spirit.
	+ His works were of duty to the Creator, not of mere good will.
	+ His works were finite and could not merit infinite glory.

• **How Christ merited**
	+ Christ merited by means of God's promises made in the law.
	+ His obedience, though performed in His manhood, was the obedience of God and man.
	+ The dignity of His works came from the Godhead.

• **The limitations of human merit**
	+ No other man can merit at God's hands, not even a worthy man like Moses.
	+ Moses could not by any work deserve life everlasting at God's hands.

• **The concept of reward as a free gift**
	+ A reward can be a free gift, as seen in Matthew 5:44 and Luke 6:32.
	+ The word "reward" can also mean "free gift" or "favor".

• **Christ's merit as the basis of reward**
	+ Christ's merit makes life everlasting a reward.
	+ Every true believer who endeavors to do the will of God is in Christ and shares in Christ's righteousness.

• **The promise of reward**
	+ A promise of reward is made to believers upon their obedience.
	+ This promise is based on Christ's merit and makes the believer's person acceptable to God.

• **The purpose of the promise of reward**
	+ The promise of reward is to allure and draw believers on in obedience.
	+ It is to encourage them to live a godly life in Christ Jesus, despite suffering affliction.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Moses' faith**
	+ Moses was called by God to lead the Israelites out of bondage
	+ He believed God would be with him and guide him in this task

• **Moses' departure from Egypt**
	+ Moses departed from Egypt twice: once after slaying an Egyptian and fleeing to Midian, and again 40 years later to lead the Israelites out of Egypt
	+ The text suggests that the second departure is the one being referred to in this passage

• **Moses' motivation**
	+ Moses' departure was not motivated by fear of Pharaoh's wrath
	+ He was motivated by faith and a sense of duty to lead the Israelites out of Egypt

• **Comparison to malefactors**
	+ Malefactors flee in fear of punishment, whereas Moses fled in faith
	+ Moses' boldness and courage in the face of Pharaoh's threats demonstrate his faith

• **Moses' interactions with Pharaoh**
	+ Despite Pharaoh's threats, Moses continued to interact with him and even returned to Egypt to deliver a message
	+ Moses' actions demonstrate his faith and trust in God's plan

• **Moses' leadership**
	+ Moses was a public person and a high magistrate, not a private individual
	+ He was a king, appointed by God to lead the Israelites out of Egypt

• **God's promise of assistance**
	+ Moses had God's promise of assistance in working strange miracles
	+ This promise gave him the courage and authority to lead the Israelites out of Egypt

• **Moses' authority**
	+ Moses was not acting on his own authority, but rather on God's command
	+ He was a king, appointed by God to lead the Israelites out of Egypt

• **Rebellion vs. obedience**
	+ Some might argue that Moses' actions were a fact of rebellion and sedition
	+ However, the text argues that Moses' actions were motivated by faith and obedience to God's command

• **Conclusion**
	+ Moses' departure from Egypt is an example of faith and obedience to God's command
	+ His actions demonstrate his trust in God's plan and his willingness to follow God's guidance.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Romish Church's Idolatry**
	+ The Romish Church creates images of God and the Trinity
	+ This is a lie, as God is invisible and cannot be represented
	+ The Bible prohibits making images of God (Exodus 20:4-5)

• **The Papists' Defense**
	+ They claim to create images of God as He has shown Himself in Scripture
	+ They point to examples of God appearing as an old man, a dove, or a burning bush
	+ However, these forms were only temporary signs of God's presence, not images to be worshiped

• **The Commandment Against Images**
	+ The Bible explicitly prohibits making images of God (Exodus 20:4-5)
	+ This commandment includes all representations of God, including the shapes used to signify His presence

• **The Impossibility of Representing God**
	+ God is invisible, making it impossible to create a true image of Him
	+ Any attempt to create an image of God is idolatrous presumption

• **Moses' Faithful Acts**
	+ Moses refused to be called the son of Pharaoh's daughter (Exodus 2:10)
	+ Moses departed from Egypt, trusting in God's promise (Exodus 4:10-12)

• **The Ordination of the Passover**
	+ Moses ordained the Passover as a sacrament of the Old Testament
	+ The Passover served as a sign of the people's temporal and spiritual deliverance

• **The Significance of the Passover**
	+ The Passover was a sacrifice, but also a sacrament that signified God's deliverance
	+ It was a sign of the people's deliverance from the bondage of Egypt and from everlasting death

• **The Meaning of the Passover**
	+ The Passover was a sign of the people's temporal deliverance from Egypt
	+ It was also a sign of their spiritual deliverance from everlasting death through the sacrifice of Christ Jesus

• **The Nature of a Sacrament**
	+ A sacrament is a sign that signifies and seals up a blessing of God to His people
	+ The Passover served as a sacrament, signifying God's deliverance to the people of Israel

• **The Passover as a Sacrifice**
	+ The Passover was called a sacrifice because it was killed and its blood was sprinkled
	+ However, it was not a sacrifice in the same sense as the sacrifices of the Old Testament, but rather a unique event that signified God's deliverance.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Passover as a Sacrifice**
	+ The Passover was a sacrifice to the Lord, where animals were burnt in sacrifice.
	+ The priests took away the animals to be consumed by fire and then gave them to the people according to their families.

• **The Passover as a Sacrament**
	+ The Passover was a sacrament, a visible sign of special blessings from the Lord.
	+ It was a sign of a double deliverance: temporal and spiritual.

• **Temporal Deliverance**
	+ The Passover signified deliverance from the destroying angel and the bondage of Egypt.
	+ The Lord promised to pass over the Israelites' houses, sparing their firstborn sons.

• **Spiritual Deliverance**
	+ The Passover also signified deliverance from the curse of the law and the wrath of God.
	+ This deliverance was a foreshadowing of the true deliverance from sin and Satan through Christ.

• **Christ as the True Passover**
	+ Paul calls Christ our Passover, sacrificed for us (2 Cor. 5:7).
	+ Christ is the true Lamb of God, set apart by God the Father before all worlds.

• **The Paschal Lamb of Men**
	+ The paschal lamb was separated by men, but by God's appointment.
	+ Christ, the true Lamb of God, was set apart by God the Father.

• **The Meaning of "Ordained the Passover"**
	+ The word "ordained" means to make or celebrate the Passover.
	+ Christ's speech to His disciples about the Passover is explained by the Evangelists.

• **The Significance of the Paschal Lamb**
	+ The paschal lamb was a sign of the Angel passing over the Israelites' houses.
	+ The phrase "killing and eating" the Passover is an improper but common way of speaking in Scripture.

• **Sacramental Union**
	+ The sign and the thing signified are united in a sacramental union.
	+ This union is not natural, but rather analogical and respective.

• **Figurative Speeches**
	+ Figurative speeches are used in Scripture to give the name of the sign to the thing signified.
	+ Examples include calling Christ our Passover and the rock Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Significance of Sacramental Signs**
	+ Sacramental signs present the thing signified to the mind of the discerning receiver.
	+ The conjunction between the sign and the thing signified arises from the institution of the Lord.

• **The Institution of the Passover**
	+ Moses ordained the killing and eating of the paschal lamb as a sign of the passing over of the Lord's Angel.
	+ The blood of the lamb was sprinkled on the doorposts of every house to signify the Angel's passing over.

• **The Meaning of the Passover**
	+ The Passover signified that the Angel of the Lord would pass over the houses with the blood sprinkled on the doorposts.
	+ The end of the sprinkling of the blood was to prevent the Angel from touching the firstborn.

• **The Importance of Faith in Sacraments**
	+ The sacraments of the New Testament must be celebrated in faith.
	+ Moses celebrated the Passover through faith, and we must do the same with the Lord's Supper.

• **The Lord's Supper as a Sacrament**
	+ The Lord's Supper succeeds the Passover in the Old Testament.
	+ It is a sign of Christ already sacrificed, and must be received in faith.

• **The Necessity of Faith in Sacraments**
	+ Faith is the hand of the soul, without which there is no receiving of Christ and his benefits.
	+ Without faith, the sacrament is unprofitable to man and unacceptable to God.

• **The Consequences of Receiving the Lord's Supper without Faith**
	+ Receiving the Lord's Supper without faith is a heavy and fearful sin that heaps up God's wrath against us.
	+ Many people sin grievously against God by receiving the Lord's Supper without understanding its nature and use.

• **The Importance of Understanding the Sacrament**
	+ Many people come to receive the Lord's Supper without knowing what the sacrament means.
	+ This ignorance is a grievous sin against God.

• **The Role of Faith in Receiving Christ's Benefits**
	+ Faith is necessary to receive Christ and his benefits.
	+ Without faith, Christ crucified is not received.

• **The Relationship between Faith and Sacraments**
	+ In every sacrament, we receive something from God, and in every sacrifice, we give something to God.
	+ Faith is essential to receive the benefits of the sacrament and to give something to God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Temporal judgments vs. eternal judgments**
	+ The text distinguishes between temporal judgments (in this life) and eternal judgments (in the afterlife)
	+ Temporal judgments are curses and hurtful, but eternal judgments are more severe

• **God's protection of the righteous**
	+ The righteous are protected from temporal judgments, as seen in Ezekiel 9:4 and Psalm 32:7
	+ Their hearts are washed in the blood of Christ, giving them freedom and security

• **Objection: God's dear children are taken away in common judgments**
	+ The text acknowledges that this is true, but notes that they are not hurt by these judgments
	+ Their hearts are sprinkled with the blood of Christ, sanctifying the judgment and affliction

• **The destruction of the firstborn in Egypt**
	+ The angel destroyed the firstborn of both man and beasts in Egypt
	+ This was a temporal judgment, taking their lives by destroying or killing their bodies

• **Abuse of the text to overthrow excommunication**
	+ Some interpret 1 Corinthians 5 as referring to the destruction of the body, not excommunication
	+ This interpretation would overthrow excommunication, but the text argues that this is not the correct understanding

• **The meaning of "flesh" in 1 Corinthians 5**
	+ When Paul opposes the flesh and the spirit, "flesh" usually refers to the corruption of the whole man
	+ The correct understanding is that the censure is for the destruction of the natural corruption and body of sin

• **The firstborn of Egypt**
	+ The firstborn of both man and beasts in Egypt were destroyed
	+ This is a remarkable judgment, as the Egyptians had previously destroyed the Israelites' children, including their firstborn

• **God's punishment of the wicked**
	+ God often punishes the wicked in their own kind with their own sins
	+ This is seen in the story of David and Uriah, as well as in the experience of those who engage in backbiting and slandering

• **The importance of considering God's judgments**
	+ The text encourages readers to consider God's judgments and be careful in their own lives
	+ This is especially important for those who have been careless in their religion

• **The significance of the blood of Christ**
	+ The blood of Christ washes away sin and gives freedom and security
	+ This is the key to understanding God's protection of the righteous and the destruction of the wicked

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Israelites' Prayer**
	+ They prayed to the Lord, acknowledging their weakness and trusting in Him (2 Chron. 20:12)
	+ Their prayer was answered, and they were preserved

• **The Red Sea**
	+ Also known as the "sea of rushes" or "sea of sedges" in the Old Testament (Ps. 106:7, 9; Jer. 49:21)
	+ Located in the Arabian Sea, between Egypt and Arabia

• **Why it's called the Red Sea**
	+ Two possible reasons:
		- Red sand on the sea floor and shore
		- Red-colored hedges and bushes that reflect onto the water

• **The Israelites' Passage**
	+ They passed through the Red Sea on dry land, without the help of a bridge or ship
	+ The waters stood like walls on both sides of them, creating a miraculous passage

• **Miraculous Nature of the Passage**
	+ The channel of the sea was like a pavement or dry land (Ex. 14:22)
	+ This was a wonderful work of God, showing the strangeness of the event

• **Criticism of the Miracle**
	+ Some people claim that Moses knew the time of the rise and fall of the waters and led the Israelites through a shallow place
	+ However, this explanation is refuted by the fact that the bottom of the sea was dry and the waters stood like walls

• **The Waters Did Not Ebb and Flow**
	+ The waters did not pass away as they normally would, but stood still on both sides of the Israelites
	+ This shows that it was a mighty and miraculous work of God, not just a natural phenomenon

• **The Egyptians' Attempt**
	+ The Egyptians, with a huge army, tried to follow the Israelites into the sea
	+ However, they were drowned, while the Israelites passed through safely

• **The Time of the Passage**
	+ The Israelites passed through the Red Sea when the Egyptians were trying to follow them
	+ This was a remarkable circumstance, as the Egyptians were attempting to do the same thing, but were unsuccessful

• **God's Sovereignty**
	+ The passage through the Red Sea shows God's sovereignty and power over nature
	+ It demonstrates that God can do miraculous things, even in the face of overwhelming odds.

• **The Power of Faith**
	+ The power of God was the principal cause of the ruin of the walls of Jericho.
	+ The downfall of the walls is ascribed to the faith of Joshua and the Israelites.

• **The Wonderful Power of True Faith**
	+ True faith can bring about impossible things, as seen in the fall of Jericho's walls.
	+ Jesus says that even a small amount of faith can move mountains (Matthew 17:20).

• **Abraham's Faith**
	+ Abraham believed God's promise that he would be the father of many nations.
	+ As he believed, it came to pass, and many nations descended from him.

• **The Gospel and Faith**
	+ The gospel is a call to all nations, and they are blessed in Christ, the promised seed of Abraham.
	+ Abraham is called the father of the faithful in all nations.

• **Overcoming Sin and Satan**
	+ Letting a man believe God's promise of salvation through faith can lead to the kingdom of sin and Satan being weakened.
	+ Saint John says that faith is the victory that overcomes the world (1 John 5:4).

• **Faith as a Cause of Change**
	+ Faith in God's promises is a principal cause of the change and overthrow of towns, cities, and kingdoms.
	+ Many men have written about the change of kingdoms, but most omit the role of faith.

• **God's Promise to Abraham**
	+ God promised to give the land of Canaan to Abraham and his seed as their inheritance.
	+ They believed this promise, and it came to pass, as seen in the fall of Jericho.

• **Faith in God's Promises**
	+ Faith in God's promises is a means for God's people to overturn cities and kingdoms that are enemies to Christ and His gospel.
	+ God has made a promise to His church that the kingdom of Antichrist will be destroyed.

• **The Destruction of Babylon**
	+ God has promised to destroy the kingdom of Antichrist, and His church believes this promise.
	+ This promise will come to pass, despite the efforts of the devil and the strength of the flesh.

• **Faith in War**
	+ When a city, town, or kingdom is to make war, faith in God's promises is a key factor in their success or failure.
	+ Faith can bring about victory, even in the face of overwhelming odds.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **The author's purpose**
	+ To provide examples of faith in the Old Testament
	+ To show that faith is a key aspect of the Christian life
* **The author's method**
	+ To provide brief examples of faith, rather than a detailed account of each person
	+ To convey the idea that there are many more examples of faith in the Old Testament than can be listed
* **The reason for the brief examples**
	+ The number of true believers in the Old Testament is very great
	+ The author wants to convey the idea that faith is a common trait among many people, rather than a rare exception
* **The difference between human and divine understanding**
	+ Humans understand things in a linear and sequential manner
	+ God understands all things at once, without distinction of time
* **The implication of the phrase "The time would be too short"**
	+ The number of believers is very great
	+ A long time would not be enough to repeat all the examples of faith
* **The answer to the question of how many will be saved**
	+ The number of the elect is small in comparison to those who will be condemned
	+ However, the number of the elect is huge and innumerable in themselves
* **The significance of the phrase "What shall I say more?"**
	+ The author is implying that there are many more examples of faith than can be listed
	+ The phrase is a rhetorical device to convey the idea that faith is a common trait among many people
* **The order of the examples**
	+ The Holy Spirit sets down the names of the persons who are examples of faith
	+ The examples are listed briefly, without a detailed account of each person
* **The significance of the examples**
	+ The examples are meant to inspire and encourage the reader to live a life of faith
	+ They demonstrate the power of faith to overcome obstacles and achieve great things
* **The overall message**
	+ Faith is a key aspect of the Christian life
	+ There are many examples of faith in the Old Testament, and many more that cannot be listed.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Order of the List**
	+ The Holy Ghost lists the individuals in a specific order, not necessarily in chronological order.
	+ The order is based on the excellence of their faith and the fruits thereof.

• **The Individuals Listed**
	+ Gideon, Barak, Samson, and Jephthah were judges in Israel.
	+ David was both a prophet and a king.
	+ Samuel was both a judge and a prophet.
	+ The prophets generally, including Elijah, Elisha, and Daniel.

• **The Reason for the Order**
	+ The Holy Ghost lists the individuals in order of dignity, not time.
	+ Those who excel in faith and the fruits thereof are listed first.

• **The Importance of Faith**
	+ The more men excel in faith, the more God will honor them.
	+ Those who honor God will be honored by Him.

• **The Characteristics of the Individuals**
	+ They were extraordinary men in their time, raised up by God for a special purpose.
	+ They were endowed with extraordinary gifts of wisdom, strength, zeal, and authority.

• **The Gifts of God**
	+ God gives extraordinary gifts to those whom He raises up for a special purpose.
	+ These gifts include wisdom, strength, zeal, and authority.

• **The Spirit of Grace**
	+ God gives the spirit of grace with a true and lively faith to those whom He raises up.
	+ This spirit of grace sanctifies their other gifts.

• **Examples of Extraordinary Gifts**
	+ The apostles were called by Christ and were furnished with extraordinary gifts of wisdom, zeal, and knowledge.
	+ They were also given the excellent grace of saving faith.

• **The Importance of Seeking Faith**
	+ We should seek to have faith and labor every day to increase our faith and obedience.
	+ The more we abound in grace before God, the more He will honor us.

• **The Purpose of the List**
	+ The list is to commend the faith of these individuals and to encourage us to seek faith and increase our obedience.

	+ Shows that true faith can come from anyone, regardless of their circumstances

• **Conclusion**
	+ The examples of Gideon, Barak, Samson, and Jephthah demonstrate that faith is not limited to those with a "good" family background or circumstances
	+ True faith can come from anyone, and is a notable commendation of their character.

	+ Some translations suggest Jephthah vowed to offer the first thing he met as a burnt offering

	+ This translation suggests Jephthah meant to dedicate the first thing he met to God

	+ This is in contrast to the Anabaptists, who say it is not lawful for Christians to make war

	+ By practicing true faith, parents can bring blessings upon the lawful means they use for their children's good

• **The Role of Faith in Times of Judgment**
	+ In times of judgment, faith is the best way to seek help
	+ True humiliation and prayer can remove curses and procure blessings

• **The Benefits of Faith**
	+ Faith brings down God's blessings from heaven upon every believer
	+ Faith is necessary for being made partaker of God's blessings in soul and body

• **The Motives for Laboring for True Faith**
	+ We desire riches, honor, favor, health, and strength
	+ We desire the favor of God, which is all in all
	+ True faith is necessary for achieving these desires

• **The Dangers of Lip-Faith**
	+ Lip-faith is not enough; we must labor for a true and lively faith in Christ
	+ Lip-faith can lead to presumption upon God's mercies

• **The Importance of Purifying Our Hearts**
	+ True faith must purify our hearts and bring forth fruit in our lives
	+ This is necessary for achieving the blessings of God

• **The Call to Labor for True Faith**
	+ We must not be content with lip-faith; we must labor for a true and lively faith
	+ This is the most important thing in the world, above all other desires and pursuits

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

* **The Importance of Faith**
	+ Faith is necessary to obtain God's blessings, both temporal and spiritual.
	+ Many people try to obtain blessings through worldly means, but fail because they lack faith.
* **The Key to God's Treasury**
	+ God's treasury is open to those who have true faith.
	+ True faith is the key that unlocks God's treasures and allows us to enrich ourselves with His blessings.
* **The Characteristics of True Faith**
	+ True faith is sound and strong, able to turn the locks of God's treasury.
	+ True faith is rare, and many people claim to have it but do not.
* **The Consequences of Lack of Faith**
	+ Those who lack faith remain sinful and unchanged, and God's judgments are rife among them.
	+ True faith is necessary for our own good, both in soul and body.
* **Examples of Faith**
	+ The author of the epistle provides examples of faith from the past, including believers under the Maccabees.
	+ These examples include people who were persecuted, mocked, and tortured for their faith.
* **The Regime of the Maccabees**
	+ The believers mentioned in this section lived under the regime of the Maccabees, before the coming of Christ.
	+ They did not have the promise of the Messiah, but waited for it by faith.
* **Persecution under Antiochus**
	+ The church of the Jews was persecuted by Antiochus, about 200 years before Christ.
	+ This persecution is recorded in 2 Maccabees 4 and 6.
* **The Source of the Narration**
	+ The author of the epistle likely gathered the information about these persecutions from stories and records of men.
	+ These records may not be extant today, but were known and available in the primitive church.
* **The Purpose of the Narration**
	+ The author of the epistle provides these examples of faith to encourage believers to labor for true faith.
	+ The examples are meant to show the power of faith in the face of persecution and hardship.
* **The Importance of Laboring for Faith**
	+ Believers must labor for true faith, which is rare and difficult to obtain.
	+ True faith is necessary for our own good, and for the power of faith to be shown in our lives.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **The Spirit of God quotes human writings**
	+ The Spirit of God quotes human writings in the Old Testament, such as the Chronicles of the kings of Judah and Israel.
	+ These quotes are not part of Holy Scripture, but rather civil or ecclesiastical stories.

• **Apostles quote human writings**
	+ The apostles, such as Saint Paul, quote human writings in their sermons and writings.
	+ They quote from Jewish writers or records that were extant and approved among the Jews.

• **Examples of quoted human writings**
	+ Saint Paul quotes from Aratus, an Athenian poet, and Menander.
	+ He also quotes from Epimenides, a Cretan poet.

• **Purpose of quoting human writings**
	+ The purpose of quoting human writings is to illustrate a point or to make a statement.
	+ It is not to establish the authority of the human writer.

• **Limitations of quoting human writings**
	+ Ministers today do not have the same guidance as the apostles.
	+ They cannot err in their public ministry, unlike ordinary ministers.

• **Sanctification of human writings**
	+ The apostles sanctified human writings by quoting them in their sermons and writings.
	+ This made them a part of Holy Scripture.

• **Ordinary ministers cannot sanctify human writings**
	+ Ordinary ministers cannot sanctify human writings by quoting them.
	+ Human writings remain human and are not God's Word.

• **Apostles were sparing in quoting human writings**
	+ The apostles were sparing in quoting human writings.
	+ They only quoted a few times in all the New Testament.

• **Apostles quoted human writings without ostentation**
	+ The apostles quoted human writings without drawing attention to themselves.
	+ They concealed the names of the authors.

• **Apostles quoted human writings for weighty cause**
	+ The apostles quoted human writings for a weighty cause or just occasion.
	+ They were persuaded in conscience that the testimonies would convince the consciences of their hearers.

• **The Church's Interchangeable State**
	+ The church of God and true believers experience periods of peace and prosperity
	+ However, they also face trouble, misery, and persecution

• **Examples from the Bible**
	+ Adam's family was God's church, initially experiencing peace
	+ However, persecution began when God accepted Abel's sacrifice and rejected Cain's
	+ Abraham's family was also a true church, experiencing both peace and adversity

	+ God's chosen people were in bondage in Egypt for 400 years
	+ They were later tried in the wilderness for 40 years and then planted in the land of Canaan
	+ The church of God experienced periods of prosperity and adversity during the time of the judges and kings

• **The Interchangeable State of the Church**
	+ The church was in subjection to surrounding nations due to its sins
	+ However, God sent mighty judges to deliver them when they cried out to Him
	+ This pattern of prosperity and adversity continued throughout the time of the judges and kings

• **The Captivity and Return**
	+ The Israelites were taken captive into Babylon after the reign of good king Josiah
	+ They were later returned by king Cyrus after 70 years
	+ After their return, they experienced periods of peace and distress

• **The Persecution of Antiochus Epiphanes**
	+ This persecution was foretold by Daniel and recorded in the books of the Maccabees
	+ It was a notorious period of persecution for the church

• **The Primitive Church**
	+ The church suffered 10 bloody and grievous persecutions in the first 300 years after Christ's ascension
	+ Between each persecution, the church experienced periods of peace and respite

• **The Heresy of Arius**
	+ This heresy denied the eternal deity of Christ and the Holy Ghost
	+ It prevailed in the church for 80 years and brought grievous persecutions

• **The Idolatry and Tyranny of Antichrist**
	+ This period of idolatry and tyranny prevailed in the church for many hundred years
	+ The church was delivered from this period about 80 years ago

• **The Church's Continued Experience**
	+ The church has continued to experience periods of peace and adversity
	+ The interchangeable state of the church is a recurring theme throughout history

• **God's Church and State**

• **Faith and Obedience**

	+ The latter is considered the better resurrection.
* **The True Meaning of the Words**
	+ The believers refused deliverance from temporal tortures and punishments because they wanted their bodies to rise again to life everlasting at the last day.
	+ They judged this to be far better than rising to a temporal life for a while in this world.
* **Lessons from the Believers' Example**

	+ This assurance should be based on faith and not just a confession of faith.

	+ This is demonstrated by Paul's example in Acts 24:15-16.

	+ The persuasion of a future resurrection to life everlasting can help overcome the natural fear of death.
	+ This fear can be stayed by the knowledge of a future resurrection.

	+ True religion and a good conscience are more valuable than all the riches and pleasures of the world.
	+ They are essential for a happy and fulfilling life.
* **The Role of Faith in Suffering**
	+ Faith in a future resurrection can give confidence in suffering.
	+ This faith can help us to endure hardships and persecutions.
* **The Benefits of a Clear Conscience**
	+ A clear conscience can give us peace and comfort in life and death.
	+ It can help us to overcome the fear of death and to live a life of true religion.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **Persecution can lead to sin**
	+ Ease and rest can lead to grievous sins
	+ Riches and promises can be more persuasive than persecution
	+ A good conscience is more easily lost to worldly pleasures than to persecution

• **Examples of suffering**
	+ Being slain with the sword (omitted as it has been discussed previously)
	+ Being destitute, afflicted, and tormented (fleeing for safety)

• **Fleeing for safety**
	+ Christians may lawfully flee for their safety in times of persecution
	+ This is not a rejection of God's hand, but rather a response to the wrath of enemies
	+ Examples: Jesus fleeing from the Pharisees, prophets fleeing from persecution

• **Objection 1: Fleeing from God's hand**
	+ Persecution can be seen as God's hand, but also as the work of wicked enemies
	+ A Christian fleeing for safety is not fleeing from God's hand, but from the wrath of enemies
	+ This is not a reason to conclude that no one should flee from persecution

• **Objection 2: Testifying faith in the face of persecution**
	+ A Christian is bound to testify their faith, but may flee if given liberty to do so
	+ God will not require a Christian to justify their faith by staying in a situation where they may be harmed

• **Objection 3: Why some Christians do not flee**
	+ Some Christians may stand firm in the face of persecution due to a special instinct or motion of God's Spirit
	+ Examples: English martyrs who stood firm in the face of persecution

• **God's sovereignty in persecution**
	+ God uses persecution as a means of trial or chastisement
	+ A Christian may flee from persecution, but still be under God's sovereignty

• **The importance of fleeing for safety**
	+ Fleeing for safety is not a rejection of God's hand, but rather a response to the wrath of enemies
	+ This is not a reason to conclude that no one should flee from persecution

• **The role of faith in fleeing**
	+ A Christian flees for safety by faith, trusting in God's provision and protection
	+ This is not a rejection of God's hand, but rather a response to the circumstances

• **The importance of reverence in judging others**
	+ We must judge reverently of those who stand firm in the face of persecution
	+ We do not know the full circumstances or motivations of others, and should not judge them harshly.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

• **God's servants can be overwhelmed with afflictions**
	+ They can be banished, driven to poverty, and deprived of goods and society
	+ They can be afflicted in body and mind, and evil-treated by all men

• **Examples of God's servants who suffered afflictions**
	+ Job, who was afflicted in body, friends, goods, and children
	+ David, who was filled with evils and vexed by God's ways

	+ The Bible promises blessings to those who fear God
	+ However, God's servants can still be afflicted with manifold calamities

• **The conditionality of God's promises**
	+ Promises of temporal blessings are conditional and subject to exception
	+ God's children may be afflicted with crosses to test their faith or chastise their sins

• **The purpose of afflictions**
	+ To test God's children's faith and graces
	+ To chastise them for sin

• **The role of faith in bearing afflictions**
	+ Faith in the promise of life in Jesus Christ can enable God's servants to bear afflictions
	+ Faith can provide a sovereign remedy against immoderate grief

• **The power of faith in overcoming afflictions**
	+ Faith can make God's servants more than conquerors in tribulation and affliction
	+ Faith can enable them to endure all estates, including hunger and want

• **The false opinion of natural and ungodly men**
	+ They judge God's servants who suffer afflictions as wicked and ungodly
	+ This opinion is false, as God's servants can still be righteous and faithful despite their afflictions

• **The importance of understanding God's ways**
	+ God's ways are not always understandable to human reason
	+ We must trust in God's sovereignty and goodness despite our afflictions

• **The hope of eternal life and blessing**
	+ God's promises of eternal life and blessing are not subject to condition or exception
	+ God's servants can still look forward to eternal life and blessing despite their afflictions in this life.

	+ The Holy Spirit says that these believers received good reports, but not the promise
	+ The promise refers to Christ's incarnation in the flesh, which they did not live to see

	+ The Holy Spirit sets down the state of the church unto the end in these examples
	+ These examples are meant to teach us to trust in God and not trust in our own strength
	+ We must remember that God's children have suffered many miseries throughout history, but have always been commended for their faith

• **Conclusion**
	+ The Holy Spirit commends the faith of believers who suffered various kinds of miseries
	+ We must learn from these examples and trust in God, even in times of adversity
	+ We must prioritize getting true faith in Christ and labor diligently to receive God's approbation.

	+ We are bound in conscience to believe in true faith, especially since we have received the promise of Christ's incarnation.
	+ This is a higher standard than that of the Old Testament believers.

• **The rarity of true faith**
	+ True and sound faith is rare in the heart, and many people claim to believe but do not.
	+ Faith is impossible without knowledge, and many people are ignorant and lack a desire to learn.

• **The connection between faith and knowledge**
	+ Faith purifies the heart and is joined with a good conscience and obedience through love.

	+ We must join practice with knowledge in obedience from a good conscience.
	+ Without this, we have no spark of true faith.

	+ A dead faith that does not produce good fruits will ultimately appear to be nothing but bare lip-faith and mere presumption.
	+ This will condemn us at the last day.

• **The importance of good works**
	+ True faith cannot be hid and will break out in good works.
	+ We must show good fruits in our lives to demonstrate the faith of our hearts.

	+ God's will and decree are not limited to good things, but also include evil things that are done by others.

	+ In the Old Testament, God provided for His church what was meet for it, but in the New Testament, He provides a better state for His church.

	+ The church in the New Testament is better than it was in the Old Testament before the coming of Christ.

	+ God has provided a better thing for us, which is a better estate for His church in the New Testament.

	+ God's prescience is not just a matter of knowing what will happen, but also includes His decree and ordination.
	+ God's prescience is not limited to good things, but also includes evil things that are done by others.

	+ God's will and decree include the sinful actions of men.
